<meta>This page serves as the preface to a scholarly edition of Irish mythological poetry, establishing the editorial methods and sources used by the author.</meta>

# PREFACE

Of the eighteen poems edited here, five have already been published: those concerning Ráth Essa, Faffand, and Almu I., in the *Todd Lecture Series*, volume 7; the poem on Inber n-Ailbine, in *Atlantis*, volume 4, page 235, from materials left by O'Curry <note>Eugene O'Curry (1794–1862), a prominent Irish social historian and philologist</note>; and the poem on Lagin II. in Stokes' *Bodleian Dindshenchas* <note>Dindshenchas: a body of early Irish literature explaining the origins of place-names</note>, page 7, and in Atkinson and Bernard's *Book of Hymns* <note>original: "Liber Hymnorum"</note>, volume 2, page 58.

As in previous volumes, I have based my text as far as possible on the *Book of Leinster* <note>A significant 12th-century Irish manuscript</note>, and I have adopted the standard practice of this manuscript <note>original: "codex"</note> as my rule for spelling <note>original: "orthography"</note>. The critical apparatus <note>The system of notes and symbols used to show variant readings from different manuscripts</note> is, I hope, reasonably thorough; the symbol "&c." is used to include various spellings of the same reading. In the text of Almu I., which is found only in the *Book of Leinster* <note>original: "L"</note>, the expanded abbreviations <note>original: "contractions"</note> are carefully italicized; this has not been done elsewhere, except in doubtful cases. I have given more weight than previously to the readings of *S₃*, a late but accurate manuscript. I have also made use of *M*, a copy which I had unaccountably overlooked until my attention was drawn to it by Professor Meyer <note>Kuno Meyer (1858–1919), a famous German scholar of Celtic languages</note>.

My thanks are due to two friends: to Professor Atkinson, who has given me constant sympathy and advice; and to Mr. W. J. Purton, who has read my proofs and helped me with useful criticism.

->EDWARD GWYNN.<-

TRINITY COLLEGE, DUBLIN,
*May, 1906.*

<summary>Edward Gwynn introduces his edition of eighteen Irish poems, detailing his manuscript sources—primarily the Book of Leinster—and acknowledging the scholars who assisted his research.</summary>
<keywords>Edward Gwynn, Book of Leinster, Dindshenchas, Irish philology, manuscript studies, Trinity College Dublin, Kuno Meyer, Eugene O'Curry</keywords>

### CITED IN THIS VOLUME.

LU = *The Book of the Dun Cow* <note>original: "Leabhar na Huidhre"</note>.

L = The Book of Leinster.

R = The Rennes Manuscript.

H = Trinity College, Dublin, H. 3. 3 <note>Manuscript catalog number 1322.</note>.

S = Royal Irish Academy, Stowe Collection, D. ii. 2.

S₂ = Royal Irish Academy, Stowe Collection, B. ii. 2.

S₃ = Royal Irish Academy, Stowe Collection, B. iii. 1.

V = Royal Irish Academy, Reeves Collection, 832.

<meta>The previous page served as a divider for Part II of the collection. This page begins the poetic entry for Rath Esa, a site associated with the legends of the High King Echu Aireman and the supernatural Midir.</meta>

## THE FORT OF ESA <note>original: RATH ESA</note>

->Here, we believe, she rested,<-
->after reaching a spirited goal:<-
->the daughter of Echu Aireman <note>original: Echdach Aireman; a legendary High King of Ireland</note><-
->and of noble Étain <note>original: Étaine áine</note>.<-

<margin>5</margin>
->Esa was the daughter's name;<-
->from her comes the name Rath Esa.<-
->A hundred of every kind of animal, without exception,<-
->were brought by her; it was a triumph of tradition <note>original: búaid mbésa</note>.<-

->Midir <note>A lord of the Tuatha Dé Danann who lived in the fairy mound of Brí Léith</note> reared that fair woman<-
<margin>10</margin>
->with the drinking of wine and mead.<-
->For nine years that girl stayed<-
->in Brí Léith—a noble arrangement.<-

->In spite of Echu Aireman,<-
->Midir carried off the festive Étain<-
<margin>15</margin>
->from Fremainn <note>The king’s royal seat, likely Frewin Hill in County Westmeath</note>, though he was of noble rank,<-
->leaving Banba <note>A poetic name for Ireland</note> in sorrow.<-

->Withered-footed Codal <note>A druid or seer in Irish mythology</note> said:<-
> "There is no need for you to search for her;
> she has been in Brí Léith from the very start;
> that is the direction she went in her courtship." <margin>20</margin>

->Alongside Echu Aireman<-
->went the hosts of noble Ireland<-
->from Fremainn—though he was of noble rank—<-
->to destroy the stronghold of Brí Léith.<-

---

<note>The following section lists variations of the text found in different historical manuscripts (identified by the letters L, R, B, Y, M, S, etc.).</note>

LRBYMSS₂S₃HV. 2. *tíchtain* (coming)] *tíchtu* in manuscript L. *bruthmar* (spirited)] *bruthmair* in manuscript S. 6. *is di* (from her)] *is de* in RBM; *uaithi*, etc. in YS₂; *is uaithi*, etc. in HS. 8. *buaid* (triumph)] *búan* (lasting) in L. *mbésa* (of custom)] LRB; *fheasa* (of knowledge) in M; *mesa*, *messa*, etc. in all other manuscripts. 9. *roalt* (reared)] *ronalt* in S₂; *dalt* in RMHSY. *ben* (woman)] *mnai* (woman/wife) in RBMS. 13. *Tar cend* (In spite of/Above the head of)] *do chinn* in L. 14. *n-ólaig* (festive/of drinking)] *eolaigh* (skilled/wise), etc. in SS₂. 15. *ciarb aireglan* (though he was of bright dignity)] LL; *gerb oreglan* in V; *ciarb airerclan* in R; *ciarboreagail* in M.

<summary>This page contains the opening stanzas of a poem about Rath Esa, explaining that the site is named after Esa, the daughter of King Echu Aireman and Étain. The verses recount how Esa was raised by the supernatural Midir in the mound of Brí Léith after Midir stole Étain away from the King.</summary>

## THE FORT OF ESA <note>original: RATH ESA; "Rath" refers to a circular earthen fort</note>

---

->Here she settled, as we believe,<-
->after reaching a goal she eagerly sought:<-
->the daughter of Eochaid Airem <note>A legendary High King of Ireland</note><-
->and of the noble Etain.<-

<margin>5</margin>
->Esa was the young woman's name;<-
->from her, the Fort of Esa takes its name.<-
->A hundred of every kind of beast, without exception,<-
->were brought by her—it was a chosen tribute.<-

->Midir <note>A lord of the Tuatha Dé Danann, a supernatural race</note> kept the beautiful woman<-
<margin>10</margin>
->providing her with wine and mead to drink;<-
->for nine years the girl stayed<-
->at Brí Léith <note>Midir’s fairy mound, located in modern County Longford</note>, serving with the spirit of a handmaid.<-

->Despite the wishes of Eochaid Airem,<-
->Midir carried off the festive Etain<-
<margin>15</margin>
->from Fremainn <note>Frewin Hill in County Westmeath, the High King’s seat</note>, even though she was of noble beauty;<-
->thus she left behind a mournful Ireland <note>original: Banba</note>.<-

->Codal of the withered foot <note>A druid or seer</note> said:<-
->"You do not need to search for her;<-
->our search begins at Brí Léith;<-
<margin>20</margin>
->for that is where she has gone for love."<-

->Alongside Eochaid Airem<-
->came the armies of noble Ireland<-
->from Fremainn—even though she was of noble beauty—<-
->to plunder the shining Brí Léith.<-

---

<note>The following notes compare word choices across different medieval manuscripts (abbreviated as L, R, B, Y, M, etc.).</note>

*Though she was of noble beauty, etc., and the rest.* 16. *until she left*] *until she left* in Manuscript L; *she left* in Manuscripts B and S; *she left*, etc. in Manuscripts R, M, Y, S₂, and H. 17. *he said*] *he said*, etc. in Manuscripts L and M. 19. *for*] *her* in Manuscripts Y and S; *after* in Manuscripts H, S₃, and V. 20. *it is that side/direction*] *it is that side*, etc. in Manuscripts H and S₂; *to that side* in Manuscript M; *it is there* in Manuscript S. *she went*] *she went* in Manuscripts L and R; *she went* in Manuscripts S and S₂; *she went* in Manuscript H. 21. *with*] *was* in Manuscript L. 23. *of noble beauty*] Manuscript L; *noble... beauty* in Manuscript Y; *of noble space* in Manuscript S; *unstable journey* in Manuscript S₂; *surrounding* in Manuscript M; *of noble beauty*, etc. the rest: *see Notes.*

<meta>This page continues the "Metrical Dindshenchas" poem regarding the "Fort of Esa" (Rath Esa). On the previous page, King Eochaid Airem had discovered that his wife Étaín had been taken to the fairy mound of Brí Léith by the supernatural being Midir, and the King had led an army there to retrieve her.</meta>

<margin>25</margin> 
->Nine years were spent at that siege;<-
->its speed was not very great.<-
->Midir was at that delay,<-
->ruining the work.<-

->After the destruction of that fairy mound,<-
<margin>30</margin> 
->there came fifty steadfast men—<-
->that group was handsome in form—<-
->to speak with the spear-wielding kings.<-

->There were brought there on a Wednesday<-
->(it was a famous story I heard)<\-
<margin>35</margin> 
->to Eochaid, in the shape of Étaín,<-
->three times fifty women <note>original: tri cóicait ban</note>, a powerful excellence.<-

->It was from those women that he chose<-
->his own truly-pure daughter <note>In the legend, Midir tricks Eochaid into choosing his own daughter, thinking she is his wife Étaín</note>;<-
->what he boasted of was a lie,<-
<margin>40</margin> 
->so that was the meeting upon which he settled.<-

->It was she who bore Meiss Buachalla,<-
->the mother of the companionable Conaire <note>Conaire Mór, a legendary High King of Ireland</note>;<-
->it was a harsh, fierce meeting:<-
->she was reared among the noble people of Éber <note>original: slúag n-Ébir; a poetic name for the Irish</note>.<-

<margin>45</margin> 
->When Eochaid went again<-
->to destroy the full Brí Léith,<-
->he took his wife after regaining her<-
->from Midir—a feat of splendor.<-

->It was then that he demanded<-
<margin>50</margin> 
->his honor-price <note>original: eneclaind; the legal compensation due to a person under Irish law based on their status</note> from Midir—<-
->the righteous, fair, and strong Eochaid—<-
->and he carried it off after a legal judgment.<-

Following King Eochaid Airem's nine-year siege of the fairy mound at Brí Léith to recover his wife Étaín, the supernatural Midir attempts to deceive the King with a test of recognition involving one hundred and fifty identical women.</meta>

# THE FORT OF ESA <note>original: RATH ESA</note>

<margin>25</margin> 
->They were nine years about that siege;<-
->its progress was not very fast.<-
->Midir was the cause of that delay,<-
->for he was busy undoing the work.<-

->After the destruction of the fairy mound,<-
<margin>30</margin> 
->there came fifty steadfast men—<-
->that group was handsome in form—<-
->to speak with the warrior kings.<-

->Then there were brought on a Wednesday<-
->(it was a famous story I heard)<\-
<margin>35</margin> 
->to Eochaid, in the shape of Étaín,<-
->one hundred and fifty women <note>original: thrice fifty women</note>, a powerful excellence!<-

->From among them he chose<-
->his own truly-pure daughter <note>In the legend, Midir tricks Eochaid into choosing his own daughter, thinking she is his wife Étaín</note>;<-
->the declaration Midir made was a lie,<-
<margin>40</margin> 
->though he claimed that was the meeting they had agreed upon.<-

->She was the one who bore Meiss Buachalla,<-
->the mother of the companionable Conaire <note>Conaire Mór, a legendary High King of Ireland</note>;<-
->it was a harsh and difficult union:<-
->she was raised to rule over the noble race of Éber <note>original: slúag n-Ébir; a poetic name for the Irish people</note>.<-

<margin>45</margin> 
->When Eochaid went again<-
->to destroy the bright Brí Léith,<-
->he took back his wife after reuniting with her<-
->from Midir—a feat of splendor!<-

->It was then that he demanded<-
<margin>50</margin> 
->his honor-fine <note>original: eneclaind; legal compensation due to a person under Irish law based on their status</note> from Midir—<-
->the righteous, fair, and strong Eochaid—<-
->and he received it after a legal judgment.<-

---

<note>The following section contains scholarly variations found in different medieval manuscripts, such as the Book of Leinster (L), the Rawlinson manuscripts (R), and others.</note>

*The others [read] forsndessid*] *forsneidseon* in manuscript M. 41-44.] omitted in manuscript S. 42. *chélig*] *chelig* in L and R; *ceblig or ceillig* in B; *ceolaig*, etc. in M, Y, and S₂; *ceillidh*, etc. in H, S₃, and V: *see Notes.* 43. *dál amnas*] L; *a famous story [scél n-irdairc]*, etc. in the others. *fuachalda*] L; *nuachalla*, etc. in R, B, S₂, and Y; *nuachalda* in H; *nuacolla*, etc. in M, S₃, and V. 44. *rosalt*] *roselt* in L. *host [sluag]*] L; *seed [síl]* in the others. *Éber*] Eogain in Y and S₂: *see Notes.* 45. *to [dia]*] L; *to [do]* in the others. 46. *Brí*] *bria* in L; *brith* in M. 52. *so he took it [conosruc]*] L; *so he met [comoruc]*, etc. in the others. *after the meeting [iar ndáil]*] L; *to the meeting [dodail]*, etc. in R and Y; *meeting [dail]* in B; *to the meeting [adail]* in S and S₂; *in the meeting [andail]* in M, H, S₃, and V.

<meta>This page continues the Metrical Dindshenchas poem regarding "The Fort of Esa" (Rath Esa). Following King Eochaid Airem's demand for an honor-fine from the supernatural Midir, the poem now details the legendary labors Midir was forced to perform and how the site of the fort was chosen by Eochaid's daughter, Esa.</meta>

# THE FORT OF ESA <note>original: RATH ESA</note>

->These are the four tasks<-
->that Eochaid Airem chose,<-

->with a multitude of shields and swords.<-

<meta>This page continues the "Rath Esa" poem from the Metrical Dindshenchas. It describes the four great "toils" or tasks that King Eochaid Airem performed as a forfeit to the fairy Midir, and the establishment of the fort (Rath) for his daughter, Esa, from which she could see three other major royal sites of ancient Ireland.</meta>

# THE FORT OF ESA <note>original: RATH ESA</note>

This is the fourfold task
that Eochaid Airem performed,
with many a distinguished company, <margin>55</margin>
and a count <note>original: "tale"; meaning a tally or counting</note> of shields and swords:

[To build] a causeway across the Bog of Lamraige,
[to clear] <note>original: "[to plant]"; the mythological task was actually the clearing or tilling of the wilderness</note> the woods growing wild over Brefne,
to clear stones from the lowlands <note>original: "Bottoms"; referring to fertile low-lying ground</note> of great Meath <note>original: Mide</note>, <margin>60</margin>
and [to spread] rushes over Tebtha <note>Modern-day Teffia, a territory in Westmeath and Longford</note>.

"O daughter, ask of me,"
said Eochaid; "tell me now
which fortress among my fortresses [you desire],
and it shall be granted to you by me." <margin>65</margin>

Then it was that she chose
the Fort of Esa <note>original: Rath Esa</note>, a precinct with a beautiful lawn;
a seat from which she might keep watch <note>The original text's "plunder" is marked with a (?) in the OCR, suggesting uncertainty; given the context of a royal seat, "overlook" or "keep watch" is a likely alternative</note>,
and from which she might see three other great fortresses:

The Mound of the Brug <note>original: Síd in Broga; the great passage tomb at Newgrange</note> of the roads, <margin>70</margin>
one of the three fortresses built correctly, fit for a hundred men,
The Mound of the Hostages <note>original: Duma Giall</note> in Tara,
and fair Dun Crimthaind <note>Crimthann's Fort</note> in Howth.

Then was [the Fort] granted
by Eochaid—a promise without deceit— <margin>75</margin>
along with everything she requested,
with plenty of treasures inside.

Midir, after the truce had expired,
came regarding the bold challenge <note>original: "award"; here meaning a settlement or claim</note>
to Eochaid once more, <margin>80</margin>
regarding the same rightful business.

---

<meta>This page continues the "Rath Esa" poem from the Metrical Dindshenchas. Having established the fort (Rath) for Esa, the poem now turns to the mythological abduction of Étáin by Midir and her residence in the fairy mound of Síd Nenta.</meta>

# THE FORT OF ESA <note>original: RATH ESA</note>

Midir <note>A king of the Tuatha Dé Danann and a powerful supernatural figure in Irish mythology</note> asked for the fair hero <note>original: "find-bile," literally "fair tree," a poetic term for a noble person</note>
regarding the swift fortress <note>original: "les"</note> that was built
for Sigmall, his daughter's son,
who dwells in the famous Mound of Nenta <note>original: Síd Nenta; a mythological fairy mound often identified with Mullaghnenta in County Roscommon</note>. <margin>85</margin>

Ogniad was his mother’s name;
she was a daughter of Midir;
her character <note>original: "láthar"; meaning her disposition or mental power</note> was not weak,
though she acted without regard for right or law.

The bright-browed Étáin was carried <margin>90</margin>
westward, though her lineage was noble,
away from the presence of Eochaid Airem,
until she remained in Síd Nenta, across the water.

There in the west, she who led many hosts <margin>95</margin>
is with Sigmall, in a fairy mound without deceit;
she stays with the grandson of Midir of great deeds,
and she has never returned here.

<meta>This page continues the poem "Rath Esa" from the Metrical Dindsenchas. The narrative follows the story of Étáin, a woman of the fairy folk (Tuatha Dé Danann) who was reborn as a mortal and married King Eochaid Airem, only to be won back and carried off by her former husband, the fairy king Midir.</meta>

# THE FORT OF ESA <note>original: RATH ESA</note>

Midir requested the noble prince <note>Midir is a king of the fairy folk; the "noble prince" refers to King Eochaid Airem.</note>
for the strong fortress where there was born
Sigmall, the son of Midir's daughter,
who dwells in the noble Fairy Mound of Nenta. <note>original: Sid Nenta; a mythological site often associated with the area around the River Shannon.</note>

<margin>85</margin> Ogniad was his mother's name;
she was the daughter of Midir.
Her character was not wicked,
though she lived without regard for rule or law.

<margin>90</margin> Étáin of the bright brows was carried away
to the West—despite her proud lineage—
from the side of <note>original: "la cend," literally "with the head," an idiom meaning "from the presence of" or "away from."</note> Eochaid Airem;
thus she remained in the Fairy Mound of Nenta across the water.

In the West is the mistress of many hosts
staying with Sigmall—a fairy place without deception—
with the brave grandson of Midir;
<margin>95</margin> and she has not returned here since.

<meta>This page transitions from the story of Étáin and the Fairy Mound of Nenta to a new poem concerning the "Brug na Bóinde" (the Boyne Valley/Newgrange). It is a praise-poem for one of Ireland’s most sacred mythological landscapes, attributed to the 10th-century poet Cináed ua Hartacán.</meta>

# THE PLAIN OF THE BOYNE I. <note>original: BRUG NA BÓINDE I. The "Brug" (modern Brú) refers to the Great Mound of Newgrange and the surrounding ritual landscape.</note>
## CINÁED UA HARTACÁN *composed this*. <note>original: cecinit</note>

Splendid is this, O plain of the Son of the Young! <note>The "Son of the Young" is Aengus Mac ind Óc, a god of the Tuatha Dé Danann associated with Newgrange.</note>
Wide is your road, traveled by hundreds;
You have covered many a true prince
of the kindred of every king that surrounds you.

Every noble wonder has adorned you, 5
O bright and pure plain, with scores of hosts;
O grassy land, rich in wagons and keen of sight,
O land of birds, of islands, and of ancient graves. <note>original: n-úag. The "land of islands" may refer to the bends and islands in the River Boyne.</note>

The house of the Son of the Young stands above your stronghold, 10
A royal ground of fair hospitality;
Indeed, over your dark waters, they offer
hostages from the fairy mounds <note>original: sídib</note> of Ireland within it.

Pharaoh's daughter <note>This likely refers to Scota, the legendary Egyptian ancestor of the Irish people.</note> lies upon your fierce ground;
A fair noble, she was a praiseworthy crown. 15
Over her, that tower yonder was raised;
Not meager is the song of praise above her head.

I see the white pool of Féic of the Warriors <note>original: Féic na Fían; a famous pool on the Boyne near Slane where the "Salmon of Knowledge" was said to be caught.</note>
To your west—no small feat of skill—
Until the day of judgment—a powerful boast— 20
It shall remain in the hollow of the king’s fortress.

A couple used to sleep here
After the battle of Mag Tuired <note>Moytura, the site of a Great Mythological battle between the Tuatha Dé Danann and the Fomorians.</note> over there:
The Great Woman <note>The "Great Woman" is often identified as the Morrígan, or perhaps Boann, the goddess of the river.</note> and the noble Dagda;
Their dwelling place there is easy to recognize.

---

*tracks* <note>original: rethaib</note>] LUB; *ranks* <note>original: srethaib</note> others. *hundreds* <note>original: cét</note>] LU; *path/treasure* <note>original: sét</note> others. 3. *you have covered* <note>original: forolgais</note>] LU; *you have granted* <note>original: dalais</note>

## THE PLAIN OF THE BOYNE I. <note>original: BRUG NA BÓINDE I. The "Brug" (modern Brú) refers to the Great Mound of Newgrange and the surrounding ritual landscape in County Meath.</note>

Splendid is it here, O plain of the Son of the Young! <note>The "Son of the Young" is Aengus Mac ind Óc, a god of the Tuatha Dé Danann associated with Newgrange.</note>
Wide is your road, traveled by hundreds;
You have covered many a true prince
Of the kindred of every king who has possessed you.

Every noble wonder has adorned you, 5
O bright, pure plain with scores of hosts,
O glowing land of grass and wagons,
O virgin meadow of birds and islands! <note>The "islands" likely refer to the bends and islands in the River Boyne.</note>

The house of the Son of the Young stands above your estate,
A royal ground of true hospitality; 10
Truly, there come above your dark stream
Hostages from the fairy mounds <note>original: sídib</note> of all Ireland to that place.

Pharaoh's daughter <note>This likely refers to Scota, the legendary Egyptian ancestor of the Goidelic (Irish) people.</note> lies upon your ground;
A kind princess; precious was her crown; 15
Over her, the tower in that place was set;
The funeral song <note>original: dirge</note> above her head was not sparing.

I see the clear pool of Féic <note>original: Fiacc. This refers to the "Pool of Féic" on the Boyne near Slane, where the Salmon of Knowledge was said to be caught.</note> of the warriors
To your west—no feeble deed—
Until the Day of Judgment—a mighty boast— 20
Shall he remain on the slope of the royal fortress. <note>original: rath</note>

Here slept a married pair
After the battle of Moytura <note>original: Mag Tuired. A famous mythological battle between the Tuatha Dé Danann and the Fomorians.</note> yonder:
The great lady and the dark <note>original: swart</note> Dagda; <note>The Dagda was the "Good God" and father-figure of the Tuatha Dé Danann.</note>
Their dwelling there is not obscure. 24

<meta>This page continues the poetic catalog of legendary burials and mythological events associated with the Brú na Bóinne landscape. It specifically mentions the "Mata" (a mythical monster) and lists several historical and legendary High Kings of Ireland buried at this site.</meta>

# THE PLAIN OF THE BOYNE I. <note>original: BRUG I.</note>

<margin>25</margin> The grave of the Mata <note>original: Máthai. The Mata was a legendary multi-legged monster or dragon of the Boyne Valley said to have been slain at the "Stone of the Mata" near Newgrange.</note> after it was slain
is clearly upon you, O Brug, where the horse-herds are dappled;
one of its bones defiled the sea,
from which fair Inber Colptha <note>original: Inber Colptha. The estuary of the River Boyne at Drogheda; legend says it was named after Colptha, son of Míl, or from the "colptha" (shin-bone) of the Mata.</note> is named.

The hide of Boadan’s enduring cow
<margin>30</margin> lies over the slope of his yellow-white stone;
the sanctuary of the faithful, keen Fian-warriors <note>original: Fían. The legendary bands of semi-independent warriors, most famously led by Finn mac Cumaill.</note>
surrounds the eastern plain of noble Nemed. <note>Nemed was a mythical leader of one of the early groups to inhabit Ireland.</note>

At the Mounds of the weak Seagulls, <note>original: Fertai na Fáilenn. A specific location within the Boyne complex.</note>
it was there the great exploit was achieved;
<margin>35</margin> a great deed of pride by the spear-point
was the slaughter by fierce Finn <note>Finn mac Cumaill.</note> that quelled the Luagne. <note>The Luagne were a warrior tribe of ancient Meath often in conflict with Finn.</note>

A pleasant son was born within you,
Cellach, <note>Cellach was the son of Cormac mac Airt, a famous High King.</note> who plundered the plain along his track;
he was fit to lead a household, yet you held him fast,
<margin>40</margin> until he died a death of pride upon you.

O prow-shaped fortress <note>original: barc brainech. Likely referring to the ship-like appearance of the mounds or their role as a "vessel" for the dead.</note> of the heavy towers,
the sea-wave visits your stronghold;
from the time of Crimthann Nia Náir to Niall, <note>Crimthann Nia Náir and Niall of the Nine Hostages were legendary High Kings of Ireland.</note>
you were the cemetery of the fair warriors.

<margin>45</margin> Fintan Feradach of the bloody journey
possessed your heavy earth;
Tuathal Techtmar, <note>Tuathal "the Legitimate," a foundational figure in Irish kingship.</note> lord of our clans—
your bare, grave-filled ground supports him.

<meta>This page continues the poetic catalog of legendary burials and mythological events associated with the Brú na Bóinne landscape. It specifically mentions the "Mata" (a mythical monster) and lists several historical and legendary High Kings of Ireland buried at this site.</meta>

# THE PLAIN OF THE BOYNE I. <note>original: BRUG I. Brug, or Brú na Bóinne, refers to the famous prehistoric ritual landscape in County Meath, Ireland, including Newgrange, Knowth, and Dowth.</note>

<margin>25</margin> The grave of the Mata <note>original: Matha. The Mata was a legendary multi-legged monster or dragon of the Boyne Valley said to have been slain at the "Stone of the Mata" near Newgrange.</note> after it was slain
is clearly visible upon you, O Brug, where horses graze;
the sea rotted its bone,
from which pleasant Inber Colptha <note>original: Inber Colptha. The estuary of the River Boyne at modern-day Drogheda. Legend says it was named after Colptha, son of Míl, or from the "colptha" (shin-bone) of the slain Mata monster.</note> is named.

<margin>30</margin> The hide of Boadan’s <note>Boadan was a legendary figure from Irish myth; his "cow" often refers to a specific landscape feature or relic.</note> immortal cow
lies over the slope of his yellow-white stone;
the sanctuary of the steadfast, fierce warriors <note>original: fian-warriors. These are the Fíana, semi-independent bands of warriors in Irish mythology, most famously led by Finn mac Cumaill.</note>
surrounds the eastern plain of noble Nemed. <note>Nemed was a mythical leader of an early group of settlers in Ireland according to the Book of Invasions.</note>

At the Trench of the gentle Seagulls, <note>original: Fertai na Fáilenn. A specific landmark within the Boyne complex.</note>
it was there the great exploit was achieved—
<margin>35</margin> a mighty feat of the spear—
the slaying of Finn whom the bold Luagne <note>The Luagne were a warrior tribe of ancient Meath often portrayed as enemies of Finn mac Cumaill.</note> warriors struck down.

Within you, a charming boy was born,
Cellach, <note>Cellach was a son of the High King Cormac mac Airt; he was killed after violating the sanctuary of the site.</note> who plundered the plains along his path;
he was strong enough to face a whole tribe, and he captured you,
<margin>40</margin> yet he died a proud death within your borders.

O prow-shaped fortress <note>original: brainech. Literally "beaked" or "having a prow," likely comparing the shape of the burial mound or the hill to a ship.</note> of the strong towers,
the sea-tide visits your site;
from the days of Crimthand Nia <note>Crimthand Nia Náir, a legendary High King of Ireland.</note> to Niall, <note>Niall of the Nine Hostages, a historical/legendary High King from the 4th or 5th century.</note>
you were the burial place of the fair-haired warriors.

<margin>45</margin> Fintan Feradach <note>A legendary figure often associated with early Irish history and the preservation of ancient lore.</note> of the bloody battles,
that powerful prince, possessed your lands;
Tuathal Techtmar, <note>A legendary High King who is credited with the consolidation of the Irish provinces.</note> the lord of our clans,
is held within your bare sepulchral soil.

<meta>This page continues the poetic catalog of the royal burials at Brú na Bóinne, transitioning from legendary monsters and early settlers to the historical High Kings of the Connachta dynasty and the eventual arrival of Christianity.</meta>

# THE PLAIN OF THE BOYNE I. <note>original: BRUG I. This refers to Brú na Bóinne, the ritual landscape in County Meath containing Newgrange, Knowth, and Dowth.</note>

<margin>49</margin> Fedlimid the Lawgiver <note>original: Fedelmed Rechtach. A legendary High King of Ireland and ancestor of the Connachta.</note> is in your reckoning, O Brug;
he was a combative warrior in every pursuit;
they are not hostile in this land,
which covers just Conn of the Hundred Battles. <note>original: Conn cét-chathach. The legendary ancestor of the leading dynasties of the north and center of Ireland.</note>

<margin>53</margin> Art <note>original: Art mac Cuinn, son of Conn.</note> did not reach you, despite his great dignity;
his followers used to range over the sloping paths;
he took a high and proud grave,
that hero of warriors, in Luachair Derg. <note>original: Luachair Deirg. A location in Munster; tradition states Art was not buried at the Boyne.</note>

<margin>57</margin> Cormac <note>original: Cormac mac Airt, one of the most famous High Kings.</note> did not reach you, though he died without misery;
the vanguard of truth—it has been verified—
he found rest above the white Boyne,
on the shore at Rosnaree. <note>original: Ros na Ríg. Tradition says Cormac rejected the pagan burial ground of Brú na Bóinne for a Christian-like burial at Rosnaree because he had glimpsed the "true faith."</note>

<margin>61</margin> Cairbre Lifechair lies upon your soil;
as does Fiacha Sraptine, noble and bright;
and Muiredach Tirech from the Hill;
and the king Eochu, father of Niall.

<margin>65</margin> Niall <note>original: Niall Noígíallach, or Niall of the Nine Hostages.</note> did not reach you—a cry that is no lie;
sad for him was the path he traveled;
after going to the Alps <note>original: Elpa. While usually meaning the Alps, in some early contexts, it referred to Scotland or Britain, but legendary accounts of Niall often place his death on the continent.</note> seven times,
the place where his grave lies is well known.

<margin>69</margin> After that, the pure faith <note>Christianity.</note> came
upon the Plain of Fál <note>original: Mag Fáil. A poetic name for Ireland.</note>; perhaps it was not too soon;
so that now everyone lies in holy cemeteries,
to separate them from iniquity and sin.

# THE PLAIN OF THE BOYNE I. <note>original: BRUG I. This refers to Brú na Bóinne, the ritual landscape in County Meath containing the great mounds of Newgrange, Knowth, and Dowth.</note>

<margin>49</margin> Fedlimid the Lawgiver <note>original: Fedelmed Rechtach. A legendary High King and ancestor of the Connachta dynasty.</note> is in your tale;
he was a warlike warrior in every pursuit;
they are not at enmity in this ground:
you hide Conn the Just, the hundred-fighter. <note>original: Conn Cétchathach. The ancestor of the northern dynasties who was said to be buried at the Boyne.</note>

<margin>53</margin> Art did not come here, though he was highest in rank,
around whom rode troops on the battlefield;
he found a grave proud and lofty,
that champion of heroes, in Luachair Derg. <note>original: Luachair Deirg. A location in Munster; tradition says Art mac Cuinn was buried where he fell in battle rather than at the royal cemetery of the Boyne.</note>

<margin>57</margin> Cormac did not come here, though he was free from sorrow:
after receiving the Truth (he affirmed it)
he found repose above the clear Boyne
on the shore at Rossnaree. <note>original: Ros na Ríg. Legend says Cormac mac Airt rejected burial at the pagan Brú na Bóinne because he had converted to a proto-Christian faith, choosing a simple grave by the river instead.</note>

<margin>61</margin> Cairbre Lifechair lies on your soil,
along with Fiachu Sraptine, noble and famous,
Muiredach Tírech from the Hill,
and the king Eochu, father of Niall.

<margin>65</margin> Niall did not come here (a cry that is not false)—
unlucky for him was the course he rowed!
After going seven times to Scotland,
the place where his grave lies was known. <note>original: Niall Noígíallach, or Niall of the Nine Hostages. He was said to have been killed abroad (on the English Channel or in the Alps), far from the ancestral burial grounds.</note>

<margin>69</margin> Thereafter came the pure Faith
to the Plain of Fail <note>original: Mag Fail. A poetic name for Ireland.</note>, a law that did not come too soon,
so that each person now lies in the burial grounds of holy men,
to sever them from wickedness and sin.

# THE PLAIN OF THE BOYNE I. <note>original: BRUG I. This refers to Brú na Bóinne, the ritual landscape in County Meath containing the great mounds of Newgrange, Knowth, and Dowth.</note>

->You hide a brave and noble company,<-

->it is hard for them where they now lie in pain.<- <note>This reflects a medieval Christian perspective: although the pagan kings were heroic, they were believed to be in Purgatory or Limbo because they lived before the arrival of Christianity.</note>

->the head of the proud descendant of Indas is away from you,<-
->even Senbecc from the Mound of noble inspiration.<- <note>original: Senbic a túaim immais áin. Senbecc was a mythical dwarf-poet; "Imbas" refers to the sacred poetic inspiration of the ancient Irish poets.</note> Noble. <note>original: Án-s. A "dúnad" or closing device where the poem ends with the same word it began with, "Án" (Noble).</note>

<margin>85</margin>

<meta>This page continues the poetic description of Brú na Bóinne, the ancient burial complex along the River Boyne. While the previous page listed the specific kings and heroes buried within the mounds, this section focuses on the spiritual fate of these pagan ancestors—viewed through a medieval Christian lens—and the enduring beauty of the landscape itself.</meta>

# BRUG I. <note>original: BRUG I. Referring to Brú na Bóinne, the "Palace of the Boyne," a major megalithic cemetery in County Meath including Newgrange.</note>

You hide a company, bold and noble,
O plain of the son of the swift Dagda! <note>The "son of the Dagda" is Aengus Mac Óg, the Irish deity associated with the mound of Newgrange.</note>
<margin>75</margin>Because they did not practice the worship of the great God,
it is the worse for them now, where they suffer in torment. <note>The medieval poet implies that despite their earthly glory, the pagan kings are in Purgatory or Hell because they lived before the arrival of Christianity.</note>

They are temporary, but you endure;
every company of the faithful travels around you.
As for the ancients, their own wisdom deceived them;
<margin>80</margin>you shall reach a noble age.

The Boyne, a place truly green and shining,
is a sounding omen... beside you
...from you, the proud grandson
of Senbecc <note>original: Senbec. A legendary dwarf and poet of the Tuatha Dé Danann.</note> from the home of noble poetry.

<margin>85</margin>War-like and magnificent is this gathering of champions!
Their strikes are swift, and their assembly is noble!
It is a sanctuary of glorious chieftains, well-trodden;
it is a home for noble-born warriors <note>original: whelps. A common poetic metaphor for fierce young fighters.</note>; it is magnificent.

---

*dosruluaid* (manuscript H); *dosrulluaidh* (manuscript M); *rosroluaidh* (manuscript S₂). &emsp; *their wisdom* (*a ngæs*)] edited version; *their custom* (*a mbés*) in the *Lebor na hUidre* (Book of the Dun Cow); *the wisdom* (*in gæs*), etc., in all other manuscripts. &emsp; 81—88] these lines appear in the *Lebor na hUidre* only. &emsp; 83. *proud* (*úabrig*)] read perhaps as the name *Ebric*: see the commentary notes. &emsp; 87. *noble* (*rán*)] spelled *ran* in the *Lebor na hUidre*. &emsp; 85. *with beauty* (*collí*)] edited version; *culli* in the *Lebor na hUidre*.

<meta>This page continues the medieval Irish poetic tradition known as the Dindsenchas ("Lore of Places"). Having established the spiritual status of the ancient inhabitants on the previous page, the poem now shifts focus to specific landmarks and legendary events associated with Brú na Bóinne (the Boyne Valley cemetery complex).</meta>

# THE PALACE OF THE BOYNE II
## SUNG BY MACNIA, SON OF OENGUS <note>original: MACNIA MAC OENGUSA cecinit. Macnia is the traditional poet to whom this poem is attributed.</note>

O nobles of Brega, a power that is no lie,
with many-pointed feats, royal is the road:
do you know the history of every tower
that is here in the Palace of the Son of the Young? <note>original: mBruig mic ind Óc. Referring to Brú na Bóinne and its legendary owner, the god Aengus Mac Óg, who was associated with the Great Mound of Newgrange.</note>

<margin>5</margin>Behold the fairy mound before your eyes,
it is clearly visible to you—it is a king’s dwelling,
built by the stern Dagda; <note>The Dagda, or "Good God," was the chief deity and father-figure of the Tuatha Dé Danann.</note>
it was a shelter, it was a fortress, wonderful in strength.

Behold the Bed of the red Dagda: <note>original: Imdai nDagdai deirg. Likely referring to a specific burial chamber or a natural feature in the landscape.</note>
<margin>10</margin>it is on the slope, without harsh violence;
he performed a noble wooing after the hunt
with a lovely woman, without decay or sorrow. <note>This refers to the union between the Dagda and the goddess Boann (the River Boyne).</note>

Behold the Two Paps of the King’s Queen <note>original: Dá Cích rígnai ind ríg. These are two hills in the Boyne Valley named for their resemblance to breasts, a common feature in Irish sacred geography.</note>
here beside the mound, toward the western plain:
<margin>15</margin>the place where lovely Cermait <note>Cermait Milbél ("Honey-mouth") was a son of the Dagda.</note> was born—
look upon the path, it is no long step.

When the wife of noble Mac Námat <note>This refers to the goddess Boann, who was married to Elcmar (here called the son of Námat) before her affair with the Dagda.</note> came
to a meeting with the swift Dagda,
and the hound followed after her,
<margin>20</margin>though it was a long journey from afar.

When Midir <note>A god of the Tuatha Dé Danann associated with the mound of Brí Léith.</note> came from Brí Léith
to rescue the weakling—it was a lucky find:
so that he brought the Son of the Young from the ford
with a shield for protection, though he was weary.

---

### THE PALACE OF THE BOYNE II <note>original: BRUG NA BÓINDE II</note>

O nobles of Brega <note>original: Breg. A region in ancient Ireland encompassing the Boyne Valley.</note>, a power that is no lie,
with skillful weapons (royal is the road):
do you know the history of every lord
that is here in the Palace of the Son of the Young? <note>original: Brug of the Mac ind Oc. Referring to Aengus, the god of love and youth, associated with Newgrange.</note>

<margin>5</margin>Behold the fairy mound before your eyes:
it is plain for you to see, it is a king’s dwelling;
it was built by the stern Dagda: <note>The Dagda (the "Good God") was the patriarch of the Irish gods.</note>
it was a shelter, it was a fortress renowned for strength.

Behold the Bed of the red Dagda: <note>original: Imdai nDagdai. A specific location or chamber within the complex.</note>
<margin>10</note>on the slope, without harsh violence;
he performed a noble wooing after the hunt
to a lovely woman free from old age <note>original: eld</note> and sorrow.

Behold the two Paps <note>A pair of hills named for their breast-like shape, often associated with goddesses.</note> of the king’s queen
here beyond the mound, west of the fairy mansion:
<margin>15</margin>the spot where lovely Cermait <note>Cermait Milbél ("Honey-mouth"), a son of the Dagda.</note> was born,
behold it on the way, not a far step;

Where <note>original: Whither</note> came the wife of the son of noble Nemed <note>Referencing the prehistoric Nemedian settlers of Ireland.</note>
to a secret meeting <note>original: tryst</note> to meet the swift Dagda,
and her dog followed her,
<margin>20</margin>though it was a long journey from afar:

Where came Midir <note>The king of the fairy mound of Brí Léith.</note> from Brí Léith
to carry off the prince—it was a lucky find;
so he carried the Son of the Young from the ford
with a shield for his protection, though he was weary.

---

<note>This section contains scholarly notes on manuscript variations. The letters refer to specific medieval Irish manuscripts (e.g., LB for Leabhar Breac, Y for the Yellow Book of Lecan).</note>

.lovely [coem], etc., LB. 14. *fairy mound of the plain [sid blai]*] sithbla RB; sirbla YS₂. 15. *place where he was born [áit ro genair]*] L; the place in which he was born [airm i ngenair], etc. in the other manuscripts [cat.]. 16. *on the path [for róen]*] L; together [maróen], etc. in the other manuscripts [cat.]. *not [ní]*] in RBS₂; *the [an]* (with *or not [vel ni]* written above) Y. *step [céim]*] omitted in S₂. 17. *to which [dia]*] to [do] S₂; from [da] M. 19. *the [in]*] the [a] RBMS₂. 20. *journey [thurus]*] theros LRM; theras Y. 21. *to which [dia]*] to [do] RHS₂. 22. *lifting/bearing [tocbáil]*] LBM; destroying [togail], etc. in the other manuscripts [cat.]. *under [fó]*] LV; under [fou] H; under [fo] in the other manuscripts [cat.]. 23. *the Young [ind Óc]*] of the Young [nOc] LYH; of the young one [in oic] M; of the Young [in oc], etc. in the other manuscripts [cat.].

<vocab>Brug na Bóinde: Brú na Bóinne/The Palace of the Boyne. Dagda: The chief deity of the Tuatha Dé Danann. Mac ind Oc: "The Son of the Young," the god Aengus. Cermait: A son of the Dagda. Midir: A powerful deity associated with the mound of Brí Léith. Nemed: A legendary leader of an early wave of settlers in Ireland. Brega: An ancient territory in East Ireland.</vocab>

<header>THE PALACE OF THE BOYNE II <note>original: BRUG II</note></header>

<margin>25</margin>After that, by a sensible arrangement,
the boy—after nine full years—
was brought to his father (it was a noble call)
to the house of the dear Dagda. <note>Referring to the return of Aengus (the Mac ind Oc) to his father, the Dagda.</note>

Preparation was made for the king
<margin>30</margin>in the fairy mound <note>original: síd</note> through a lasting deception;
from this (it is no hidden secret)
comes the Mound of the Strong <note>original: Duma Treisc</note> before the eyes of the crowds.

Afterward, the stern Dagda departed
from his fort—it was no house of sorrow—
<margin>35</margin>and stayed in Ochan <note>original: Ochain. A place near Tara where the Dagda was said to have died after being wounded in the Battle of Moytura.</note>, a time of lamentation,
after the suffering of the host, after the hour of drinking.

The Grave-mounds of Esclám <note>original: Ferta Escláim. Esclám was the Dagda's herald.</note>, a noble journey,
where good men used to cast their questions;
a plot of land with a strong portion, an act without concealment;
<margin>40</margin>it was a path of grace for the son of Calprainn. <note>original: mac Calpraind. This refers to Saint Patrick, whose father was named Calpurnius, suggesting the site's later Christian significance.</note>

Do you know the Cave of the Cattle-pen <note>original: Derc mBuaile</note>,
the heir of evil <note>original: fí. Suggesting a site of supernatural danger or "poison."</note> around the plain?
From it, you drank a swift draught—
a drink for the host, in the presence of a pure hero.

<margin>45</margin>Do you know the Grave of fierce Cellach <note>original: Lecht Cellaig. Cellach was a son of Máel Cobo, a 7th-century High King; his inclusion shows the site's importance across centuries.</note>
with a cry of pain? The wind was filled with sound:
he died by a swift heroic deed
when he was in the north on a wild hunt.

---

<margin>25</margin>Afterward, the boy was brought—
on that day nine full years later—
to his father, by a clever agreement;
it was a short-lived joy
for the beloved Dagda <note>The Dagda: A primary god of the Tuatha Dé Danann, often associated with fertility, agriculture, and strength.</note> at his house.

<margin>30</margin>He provided entertainment for the King
in the mound by means of a lasting deception;
from this comes the name—it is no mystery without a key—
the Mound of Strength <note>original: Duma Treisc</note> before the eyes of the hosts.

<margin>35</margin>Afterward, the stern Dagda yielded his claim,
he who once owned the keep—it was no house of grief:
so he lived in Ochan <note>original: Ochain. A place near Tara where the Dagda was said to have retreated or died.</note>, a journey of lamentation,
after the labor of war, after a time of feasting.

<margin>40</margin>The trench of Esclám <note>Esclám: The Dagda's herald or messenger.</note>, a revered pilgrimage site,
where good men used to ask questions;
a field with a brave portion, a deed without concealment;
it was a path of grace for the son of Calpurn. <note>original: mac Calpuirn. This refers to Saint Patrick, whose father was Calpurnius, marking the Christianization of the site.</note>

Do you know the Well of Bualc the Good,
and his successor throughout the plain,
from which he drew a drink . . . .
a drink for the host, in the presence of a bright hero.

<margin>45</margin>Do you know the Grave of fierce Cellach <note>Cellach: Likely referring to Cellach son of Máel Cobo, a 7th-century High King of Ireland.</note>
surrounded by wailing in unison? The breeze was filled
with the sound:
he died at the hands of a swift, heroic pair,
when he was in the north on a foolish, unwise act.

<meta>This page begins a new section of the Dindsenchas (The Lore of Places) concerning Inber n-Ailbine, shifting from the monuments of the Boyne Valley to the legendary history of the Delvin River estuary.</meta>

### THE ESTUARY OF THE AILBINE <note>original: Inber n-Ailbine; the mouth of the river Delvin, which forms the border between County Dublin and County Meath.</note>

O men of Muired <note>original: Muirid; the people of the territory surrounding the estuary</note>, of bright honor,
whose swiftness is not easy to count,
I will tell you in my warm dwelling
the clever history <note>original: senchus; traditional Gaelic lore or history</note> of Ailbine.

<margin>5</margin>Long ago there was one of high renown,
over Ireland <note>original: Fótla; a poetic name for Ireland, derived from a goddess of the Tuatha Dé Danann</note> without division:
he was the leader of every noble fleet in his day,
Ruad son of Rigdonn, the very bold.

He reached a path of prosperity, a choice without equal,
<margin>10</margin>over the good surface of the calm, early sea,
to speak with his friend, Gall:
it was a very strong journey to Scandinavia <note>original: Lochlaind</note>.

He went with three ships, a famous brightness,
they were vessels of constant strength:
<margin>15</margin>they were brought to a standstill—a cause of sorrow—
upon the surface of the open sea.

They were unable to move in any direction;
the stubborn delay was hard;
into the great, noble sea without fail
<margin>20</margin>went Ruad the noble, the choice-striker <note>original: roguinech; meaning an expert or choice wounder/slayer</note>.

---

<meta>This page continues the poem regarding the legendary history of the Estuary of the Ailbine (the mouth of the Delvin River). The narrative introduces the hero Ruad, son of Rigdonn, whose voyage to Scandinavia is mysteriously halted in the middle of the ocean.</meta>

### THE ESTUARY OF THE AILBINE <note>original: Inber n-Ailbine</note>

O men of Muired <note>original: Muirid; the people of the district</note>, of bright honor
among any headstrong company!
In my warm dwelling, I shall tell you
the clever story of Ailbine.

<margin>5</margin>There was once a traveler of great fame who rowed
north of undivided Ireland <note>original: Ériu</note>:
in his day, he was the leader of every brilliant band,
Ruad, the son of the valiant Rigdonn.

<margin>10</margin>He set out on a prosperous journey—a choice beyond dispute—
over the fine crests of the sea, early and late;
it was a truly brave voyage to Scandinavia <note>original: Lochlainn</note>
to speak with his friend, the Norseman.

He arrived with three ships, splendid and bright;
they were vessels of enduring power;
<margin>15</margin>yet they were brought to a sudden halt (how great the sorrow!)
upon the shoulders of the open sea.

They had no power to move in any direction;
the restraint was firm and strong.
Into the mighty ocean, without hesitation,
<margin>20</margin>went the noble Ruad, the striker <note>original: roguinech; meaning a choice wound-inflicter or warrior</note>.

---

<note>The following notes are textual variations found in the original Irish manuscripts.</note>

*Line 12: Norway (Lochlainn)*: fa Y. *rabaile*] YM; rabaile B; robaile R; robale **the others** <note>original: cæt.; abbreviation of the Latin "caeteri," meaning "the others" or the remaining manuscripts</note>. **13.** *nóithib*] noitib RB; næithe YS2; naithe, etc. SS3; noite H; noiche M. *noithech*] othech S2; næthech S. **14.** *soithech*] soigteac B; sæthrach Y; sætrach SS2. *sír*] sith RB. **15.** *duba*] doba H; dobo S2. *domna*] domnu RYM. **17.** *fimidset*] femid B; feimdis M. *lúd as*] luth ar YS; lathar S2; lúth as, etc. **the others**. *nach*] cach, etc. YSS2M. *robo dúr*] robádur R. **18.** *dron*] droch RBS2. *fuirech*] suideach M. **19.** *isin*] forsin YSS2. **20.** *-guinech*] fhuilech R.

### THE ESTUARY OF THE AILBINE <note>original: Inber n-Ailbine</note>

When he hastened indeed to release the ship 
through the salty depths of the sea’s treacherous waters,
he found, in the secret spot to which he swam,
nine female forms, beautiful and firm.

<margin>25</margin>
They told him in pure, clear voices
that it was they who had delayed him:
. . . . . . . . . . . . . . <note>Lines missing from the original manuscript</note>

<margin>30</margin>
Nine of those women, excellent and powerful;
it was difficult to reach them <note>original: cruadach; suggesting they were either physically hard to approach or formidable in character</note>.

He slept nine nights with the women
without sorrow, without tearful mourning,
<margin>35</margin>
beneath the waveless sea
on nine beds of bronze <note>Bronze beds or ships are common symbols of the Otherworld in Irish mythology</note>.

Although one of the women was pregnant by him
—it was a momentary exchange <note>original: malart mithise; implies a temporary distraction or trickery</note>—
he departed from them without any ill-will
<margin>40</margin>
on the condition that he should return again.

When she had released him to his noble comrades,
he rowed with the crews of his powerful fleet:
it was a fortunate start, and in a short time
he reached Norway <note>original: Lochlaind; often used in Irish texts to refer to Norway or Scandinavia</note> of noble courage.

<meta>This page continues the narrative of Ruad, son of Rigdonn. After a seven-year stay in Norway (Lochlann), Ruad is returning to Ireland but has broken his promise to return to the supernatural women he met in the depths of the sea.</meta>

<page-num>31</page-num>

### THE ESTUARY OF THE AILBINE <note>original: Inber n-Ailbine; refers to the mouth of the River Delvin on the coast of County Dublin and Meath</note>

<margin>45</margin>
When they arrived in the east across the sea
with good fortune and great renown,
they remained for seven years seeking fame
with his victorious friend.

<margin>50</margin>
Afterward, Ruad "of the Spears" went on his way
across the waters—that noble youth, keen and slender—
from the east over the strong, pure billows of the sea,
until he reached the level plain of Ireland <note>original: Erin</note>.

The rightful prince was unfaithful;
it was not a just decision nor an honorable act
<margin>55</margin>
to fail to return to the women across the smooth water,
in the same way he had promised.

When the noble leader reached land in the south
at the plain of Muired <note>original: Mag Muireda; a territory likely in the southeast of Ireland</note> with its lowland homesteads,
possessing a clear reputation for stern strength,
<margin>60</margin>
men heard a war-like song.

That was the song of those melodious women
with their pure, mellow, and sweet-sounding speech,
as they pursued Ruad "of the Spear-point"
over the restless, clear-streaming tide.

<margin>65</margin>
They sailed a boat made of flawless metal <note>original: cred; often refers to bronze or a precious alloy</note>,
(it was no... <note>The text here contains an ellipsis or unclear original meaning regarding the "black hull of mourning"</note>)
nine of them, fierce, radiant, and bright,
to the high Estuary of the Ailbine.

->An evil deed was committed there<-

<summary>This page concludes the tragic tale of Ruad, describing how one of the supernatural women killed Ruad's son (and her own) in revenge for his broken promise, giving the River Ailbine its name from the "great crime" (oll-bine). It also offers alternative folk etymologies for the surrounding plain involving Muiredach son of Cormac and Muiriath wife of Labraid.</summary>

--- Page 45 ---
<meta>This page continues the verse account of the "Dindsenchas" (lore of places) for Inber n-Ailbine. It concludes the tragic story of Ruad's son and offers alternative folk-etymologies for the surrounding plain.</meta>

<page-num>33</page-num>

# THE ESTUARY OF AILBINE (INBER N-AILBINE)

<sig>D</sig>

Then one of those women committed an evil deed, 
<margin>70</margin> bearing no light burden <note>This likely refers to the weight of her grief or the child she carried.</note>: 
she killed the son of Ruad, who was strong and good— 
and he was her very own son.

She made a throw with her son—a crime worse than any other 
(it was a mark of shame upon his house on earth); 
<margin>75</margin> she hurled him out during the bright clash of battle 
so that he met his death.

Then that loud-voiced crowd cried out, 
the host whom the fierce Ruad led, 
all of them shocked by the blatant crime: 
<margin>80</margin> "Dreadful, dreadful was the deed!"

From this event comes the name—a title free from envy— 
of the river, whose fame we do not hide, 
just as we tell it to you, O men!

<margin>85</margin> If the name of your plain (a source of pleasant pride!) 
is the long-held title free from the reproach of battle, 
let it be named after the <term>stout pillar</term> <note>A metaphor for a strong warrior or supporter of the community.</note> 
Muiredach, son of Cormac.

Or if the learned prefer 
<margin>90</margin> to look toward glorious deeds of pride, 
they shall call it *Builiath* <note>original: Bái-iath; literally "Cow-land"</note>, a wealth that is not silent, 
named after Moriath, the wife of Labraid.

---

<header>THE ESTUARY OF AILBINE (INBER N-AILBINE)</header>

->Labraid the Exile <note>original: *Labraid Loingsech*; a legendary High King of Ireland said to have been exiled to Gaul, returning with a foreign army to reclaim his kingdom.</note>, with his fearsome strength,<-
->came here with a vast and well-armored host;<-
<margin>95</margin>->a lord of excellence over the plain of kine <note>original: *fuil-iath*; likely a variation of *bái-iath* (cow-land), referring to the wealth of the territory.</note>, a man of strife,<-
->his wife was the youthful Moriath.<-

->Great Moriath earned the tribute of my verse;<-
->with a great host, she cleared the forests:<-
->when the journey was begun, she was not fated for death,<-
<margin>100</margin>->as it is told to you, O men.<-

---

### Textual Variations and Notes

94. **tadchaid** <note>original: "he came"</note>] Manuscript B; **tadclaid** Manuscript Y; **tadcclaith** Manuscript H; **tadcloidh** Manuscript S; **tadcloich** Manuscript S₂; **tachlaidh** Manuscript M; **taga** Manuscript R; **tadhglaith** Manuscripts S₃ and V.
<margin>95. **fi** <note>original: "king" or "lord"</note>] **faoi** Manuscript H; **fri** Manuscripts R, S, and S₂.</margin>
**fuiliath** <note>original: "blood-plain" or "kine-plain"</note>] **fuilliath** Manuscript R; **fuilliat** Manuscript B; **foliath** Manuscripts Y, S, and S₂. **fer** <note>original: "man"</note>] **fri** Manuscript M. **facht** <note>original: "strife" or "fighting"</note>] **fecht** Manuscripts R and S₂.

<summary>This page concludes the poetic history of Inber n-Ailbine by introducing Labraid the Exile and his wife Moriath, who are credited with clearing the local forests and establishing the prosperity of the "plain of kine."</summary>

--- Page 47 ---
<meta>This page concludes the verse account of the "Dindshenchas" (lore of places) for Inber n-Ailbine. It finalizes the story of Labraid the Exile and his wife Moriath, followed by scholarly notes comparing variations found in different medieval manuscripts.</meta>

# THE ESTUARY OF AILBINE (INBER N-AILBINE)

->Labraid the Mariner <note>original: *Labraid Loingsech*; a legendary High King of Ireland who was said to have been exiled to Gaul and returned with a foreign army.</note>, with his mighty limbs,<-
->came with a vast host of mail-clad warriors.<-
<margin>95</margin>->...upon the blood-stained plain, he was a man of war;<-
->his wife was the youthful Moriath.<-

->Moriath earned great honor regarding this place,<-
->among the host that cleared these forests.<-
->When she visited it, she brought no misfortune,<-
<margin>100</margin>->as is recounted, O men!<-

---

### Textual Variations and Manuscript Notes

96. **ba hi** <note>original: "she was"</note>] **hi** in Manuscript B; **is a** in Manuscript M. **macdacht** <note>original: "youthful" or "maidenly"</note>] **macacht** in Manuscripts R and H; **mactacht** in Manuscript M.
98. **ro** <note>a grammatical particle</note>] **re** in Manuscripts R and B.
99. **ro** <note>a grammatical particle</note>] **no** in Manuscript M. **triallad** <note>original: "journeyed" or "attempted"</note>] **triall** in Manuscript S₂. **nirbo** <note>original: "was not"</note>] **nibo** in Manuscripts Y and S.
100. **adfiadar** <note>original: "is told"</note>] **dofiadfraid** in Manuscript R; **dofiadar** in Manuscripts B, M, S₂, and S₃.

# OCHAN
<note>Ochan is the legendary burial place of Niall of the Nine Hostages, typically identified as being near Teltown in County Meath.</note>

## COMPOSED BY CINAED ÚA HARTACÁN
<note>original: *Cinaed úa Hartacán cecinit*; Cinaed was a prominent Irish poet who died in 975 AD.</note>

Behold the burial mound of warlike Niall,
over the grave of a man fierce, relentless, and hardy:
here on the edge of the road of the many hosts,
he has taken his cold rest in the earth.

<margin>5</margin> Niall, son of Eochu, whose tomb this is,
went seven times across the surging sea:
he extended the heritage of Conn <note>original: *comarbas Cuind*; refers to the High Kingship of the northern half of Ireland, traditionally associated with the ancestor Conn of the Hundred Battles.</note>
until he was slain upon the surface of the English Channel <note>original: *muir n-Icht*; the sea between England and France.</note>.

When the stern company called out from the rampart,
<margin>10</margin> "It is our wish to see the king who rightfully rules us,"
the prince rose up after that—
the being who was the proudest under heaven.

Eochu was the name of the man,
of the numerous Leinstermen, with a deadly hand;
<margin>15</margin> into the side of Niall of the bright shoulder,
he thrust a spear in the sight of all the armies.

Though that Leinsterman went across the sea
to join the battle of the fierce and stingy Saxons,
the slaying of the king during that great voyage
<margin>20</margin> was a strange and terrible deed to perform there.

Whenever a storm or hardship came upon them,
he was raised up—a powerful treasure;
it was the "Truth of a Prince" <note>original: *fír flatha*; a concept in Irish mythology where the physical presence or righteousness of a king brings luck and victory.</note> even after the encounter with death:
seven battles were won by simply facing his countenance.

---

### Textual Variations and Manuscript Notes
<note>These notes refer to various medieval Irish manuscripts: L (Book of Leinster), R (Rawlinson B 506), B (Book of Ballymote), Y (Yellow Book of Lecan), M (Book of Lecan), H (Trinity College Dublin H.3.3), S/S₂/S₃ (Stowe manuscripts), and V (Royal Irish Academy 23 L 22).</note>

**1.** **burial mound** <note>original: *ferta*</note>] **grave** <note>original: *fert*</note> in Y, H, S₂, S₃; **tomb** <note>original: *firt*</note> in S. **2.** **grave of a man** <note>original: *lecht fir*</note>] and so forth in S, S₂; **burial place** <note>original: *leachtaigh*</note> in M; **hillside** <note>original: *lettir*</note> in others. **3.** **here on the edge** <note>original: *sund for ur*</note>] **right here upon** <note>original: *sunna for*</note> in Y; **here upon** <note>original: *sund for*</note> in S₂. **4.** **cold** <note>original: *n-úar*</note>] **chilly** <note>original: *uar*</note> in L, B. **5.** **whose** <note>original: *assa*</note>] **whose is** <note>original: *isa*</note> in Y, H, M, S, S₂, S₃. **6.** **sea** <note>original: *tricht*</note>] **quick** <note>original: *tricc*</note> in L. **7.** **extended** <note>original: *roreraig*</note>] **subdued** <note>original: *coriaraidh*</note> in S₂. **8.** **upon** <note>original: *con*</note>] **by** <note>original: *ro*</note> in B. **surface** <note>original: *muing*</note>] **wave** <note>original: *tuind*</note> in M. **sea** <note>original: *mara*</note>] **the sea** <note>original: *mar h-*</note> in H. **9.** **said** <note>original: *ráid*</note>] **was said** <note>original: *ráded*</note> in L; **says** <note>original: *raidhi/raidid/raidhit*</note> in R, M, Y, S, S₂, S₃. **the** <note>original: *in*</note>] omitted in Y, S₂, S₃. **from the** <note>original: *din*</note>] **of the** <note>original: *di*</note> in L; **to the** <note>original: *do*</note> in S₂. **rampart** <note>original: *mur*</note>] in L, S₂; **sea** <note>original: *muir*</note> in all others. **10.** **wish** <note>original: *ail*</note>] **desire** <note>original: *áill*</note> in L. **king** <note>original: *ríg*</note>] **the king** <note>original: *in rig*</note> in L. **who rules us** <note>original: *nondlig*</note>] **who ruled us** <note>original: *rondlig/rodlig*</note> in R, B, M, Y, H, S, S₂, S₃. **11.** **after that** <note>original: *iarsein*</note>] **since then** <note>original: *arsain*</note> in B. **12.** **was** <note>original: *ba*</note>] **is** <note>original: *is*</note> in M; **were** <note>original: *bat*</note> in S₃. **13.** **it was** <note>original: *hed*</note>] in L, H; **it** <note>original: *he*</note> in all others. **15.** **with** <note>original: *con*</note>] **to his** <note>original: *coa*</note> in R, S₃. **bright** <note>original: *glain*</note>] in L; **white** <note>original: *gil*</note> in R, S₃; **shining** <note>original: *ngil*</note> in all others.

<summary>This poem by Cinaed úa Hartacán details the death and burial of the legendary High King Niall of the Nine Hostages, describing how he was killed by a Leinsterman across the sea and how his body continued to lead his army to victory.</summary>
<keywords>Niall of the Nine Hostages, Niall mac Echach, Ochan, Cinaed úa Hartacán, Sea of Icht, Eochu, High King of Ireland, Irish mythology</keywords>

--- Page 49 ---
<meta>This page continues the "Dindshenchas" (The Lore of Places) poem concerning Ochan. It picks up from the previous page's description of the death of the High King Niall of the Nine Hostages and the miraculous power of his body in battle.</meta>

( 37 )

# OCHAN
<note>Ochan is the legendary burial place of Niall of the Nine Hostages, traditionally located near Teltown in County Meath.</note>

Behold the warlike burial-trench of Niall
over the grave of a man fierce, relentless, and strong;
here, by the side of the path used by the armies,
he has found a cold bed in the soil.

<margin>5</margin>
Niall, son of Eochu, whose grave this is,
went seven times swiftly across the great sea;
he ruled the heritage of Conn <note>original: *comarbas Cuind*; the legacy of Conn of the Hundred Battles, a legendary High King of the northern half of Ireland.</note>
until he was slain above the surf of the Ictian Sea <note>original: *muir n-Icht*; the medieval Irish name for the English Channel.</note>.

When the stern people spoke from the rampart,
<margin>10</margin>
“We desire to look upon the king who rightfully rules us,”
the prince rose up then and stood erect—
the man who was the proudest under heaven.

Eochu was the name of that man,
one of the numerous Leinstermen, with a hand of poison;
<margin>15</margin>
into the side of Niall of the white shoulders,
he drove his spear in the sight of all the hosts.

Though the Leinsterman achieved this deed yonder,
in alliance with the violent and greedy Saxons—
the killing of the king during his great voyage—
<margin>20</margin>
it was a strange miracle that was performed there.

Whenever trouble or danger came upon them,
his body would be raised up (a powerful treasure);
it was the act of a true king even after the sentence of death:
the winning of seven battles in his presence <note>According to legend, Niall's army carried his corpse before them into battle, and the sight of their dead king ensured victory in seven subsequent engagements.</note>.

---

### Textual Variants and Manuscript Readings
<note>The following notes represent the scholarly "critical apparatus," tracking how different historical manuscripts (labeled with letters like L, B, R, M) vary in their spelling and word choices for this poem.</note>

16. **In the presence of** (*fiad na*)] *fiadh ua* in Manuscript M; *fiana* in S. **Armies** (*sluagaib*)] *sluaga* in Y.
18. **In battle** (*fri báig*)] *ri báig* in L; *fri baid* in B; *a mbaig*, etc., in R, Y, H, S<sub>2</sub>, S<sub>3</sub>; *in baigh* in S; *combaid* in M. **Fierce** (*ngalbech*)] *nailbech* in M.
19. **From the voyage** (*dond imram*)] *dind iram* in R; *di ammram* in B; *ba himrall* in S<sub>2</sub>.
20. **Deed** (*glond*)] *in glonn*, etc., in R, M, H. **Was done** (*doringned*)] *doigneadh* in B; *rognid* in R; *dognidh* in M.
21. **When** (*Tan*)] *in tan* in L. **It reached them** (*nosticed*)] *dosfiged*, etc., in R, B, M, S<sub>3</sub>; *doficced* in H; *. . ficed* in Y; *rosfighed*, etc., in S, S<sub>2</sub>. **Hardship** (*cith*)] in L; **Battle** (*cath*) in all other manuscripts.
22. **Was raised** (*tocabtha*)] *ba tocabtha* in L; *dogaibthea* in S; *tocbaidis* in S<sub>2</sub>; *tocfaithea* in M. **Strong** (*serig*)] in L; *seric* (with *or strong* written above) in B; *sethnach*, etc., in Y, H, S, S<sub>3</sub>; *seirech* in R, M, S<sub>2</sub>. **Treasure** (*dúis*)] *tuis* in Y, H, S, S<sub>2</sub>.

<summary>This page concludes the poem on Ochan, describing how the High King Niall was assassinated by a Leinsterman while abroad and how his body was used as a powerful talisman to win seven battles after his death.</summary>
<keywords>Niall of the Nine Hostages, Ochan, Eochu, Heritage of Conn, Ictian Sea, Saxons, Dindshenchas, manuscript variants, medieval Irish royalty</keywords>

--- Page 50 ---
<meta>This page continues the "Metrical Dindshenchas" poem concerning Ochan. It describes the aftermath of the High King Niall's death, the release of his foreign hostages, and his burial at Ochan, where the four provinces of Ireland gathered to mourn him.</meta>

# OCHAN

<margin>25</margin>
Niall spoke a true word
when he was struck upon the sea by a secret plot,
at the place where Niall's tomb <note>original: *ilad*; a grave, tomb, or stone monument.</note> was built,
that his hostages should be sent back home.

Because of this, they were released
<margin>30</margin>
across the grey, windy sea—a heavy struggle;
the hostages of the Saxons—a great and fine assembly—
the hostages of the Franks, and the hostages of the Romans from the south.

The band of his strong, well-armed household
went westward from Tara <note>original: *Temair*; the seat of the High Kings of Ireland.</note>;
<margin>35</margin>
because of it, there was the sorrow of beating palms:
*The great Ochan of Niall’s people.* <note>The name Ochan was traditionally explained as coming from "ochán," an exclamation of grief or groaning.</note>

In noble Ochan, they took leave
of one another in a vast ceremony:
the men of Leinster and Munster (whom it grieved),
<margin>40</margin>
the men of Connacht and Ulster—men of fame and great retinues.

The warrior who had united them was a king;
his strength was never weak in this world;
Niall’s time was short—he who was a sanctuary of protection—
until the beloved faith of God arrived. <margin>D.</margin> <note>"D" likely refers to the Dindshenchas collection or a specific manuscript source.</note>

<margin>45</margin>
His sons divided afterward
the Island of Art <note>original: *inis n-Airt*; a poetic name for Ireland, referring to the legendary High King Art mac Cuinn.</note>—they were illustrious men;
it is to them that hostages are given
as long as clouds remain around the bright sun.

---

<note>The following section provides academic variant readings from different manuscripts such as the Book of Leinster (L), the Yellow Book of Lecan (Y), and others.</note>

25. *Firian*] true, &c. YMSS₂. 26. *rian*] sea-path L.; third part M. *tria*] without LS₂. 27. *áitt*] &c. LMS₂; place cæt. *i clanta*] where was planted H; will be planted S₃; was built S₂. *ilad*] tomb, &c. YS₃; lamentation H. 28. *co teilctis*] they used to leap L. *ngéill*] hostage B; hostages RMS₂. *for*] L; on cæt. 29. *aire*] therefore YS; therefore S₂; for M. *ro léicthea*] they were let go L; they were released, &c. cæt. 30. *gaeth*] windy RY; the windy H. *rian*] LH; omitted in others: see Notes. *nglass*] grey BMS₂S₃. *trom*] editor; was heavy [ba trom] in the codices. 31. *mass*] omitted in L. 35. *bói*] was L. *iar mbaisse*] after hand-beating L; after beauty Y; after madness, &c. HS₃. 36. *mór*] great, &c. BYMS₃.

<summary>This page describes the funeral of Niall of the Nine Hostages at Ochan, noting his dying command to release his foreign captives and the gathering of the four provinces of Ireland to mourn him.</summary>
