--- Page 51 ---
<meta>This page continues the poem regarding Ochan and the burial of the High King Niall of the Nine Hostages. It details the release of his foreign captives and the mourning of the four provinces of Ireland at his funeral site.</meta>

# OCHAN

<margin>25</margin>
Niall spoke a just word
when he was killed at sea by treachery,
at the spot where Niall’s tomb was built,
decreeing that his hostages should be sent home.

<margin>30</margin>
Afterward, they were set free
across the green, stormy sea (wild was its warfare);
the hostages of the Saxons (they were a large and handsome company),
hostages of the Franks, and hostages of the Romans from the south.

The warrior band of his powerful, warlike retinue
<margin>35</margin>
came westward from Tara;
it was named thereafter, following grief and the beating of breasts,
*Great Ochan of the followers of Niall.* <note>The name Ochan was traditionally linked to "ochán," an Irish expression of mourning or lamentation.</note>

In noble Ochan, they took leave
of one another, the most noble in rank:
the men of Leinster and Munster (it caused them grief),
<margin>40</margin>
the men of Connacht and Ulster—famous men and armies.

A hero who was king had united them;
his strength was not weak in this world.
It was only a short time after Niall (whose fame was a shelter)
until the blessed Faith of God arrived. <note>This refers to the arrival of Christianity in Ireland shortly after Niall's reign.</note>

<margin>45</margin>
His sons afterward divided
the Island of Art <note>original: *Inis n-Airt*; a poetic name for Ireland, referring to the legendary High King Art mac Cuinn.</note>, who was a marvelous man;
it is to them that hostages shall be given
as long as clouds shall surround the bright sun.

---

### Variant Readings <note>original: *Apparatus Criticus*; a scholarly list of differences found in various original manuscripts.</note>

37. *parted*] "they celebrate" in manuscripts BYMSS₃; "he celebrated" in RH; "celebration" in S₂.
38. *vast*] variations of "huge" or "great" in BYM.
39. *it grieved them*] variations in L and S₂.
40. *men*] "at" or "with" in L. *fame and*] "fame is" in R.
41. *united them*] variations in RB, M, and HSS₂.
42. *was not*] variations in L and S₂. *on*] "upon" in YB. *pillar/hero*] variations in M. *on*] "upon" in RYS₂.
43. *shelter*] "a shelter" in L and B; "the shelter" in R.
44. *arrived*] variations in LH, S₂, and others. *Faith of God*] "dear God" in RBMS.
45. *divided*] "they divided" in L.
46. *men*] variations in LM, BYSS₂S₃, and RH.
47. *hostages*] "pledge" in H.
48. *so long as shall be*] variations in LR, M, and others. *clouds*] variations in LR, BHSS₂S₃, Y, and M. *around*] "in" in B.

<summary>This page concludes the account of Niall of the Nine Hostages' funeral at Ochan, describing the release of his international captives and the subsequent division of Ireland among his sons. It emphasizes Niall's role as a powerful unifier just before the arrival of Christianity.</summary>
<keywords>Niall of the Nine Hostages, Ochan, Tara, Irish Mythology, Saxons, Franks, Romans, Island of Art, High Kings of Ireland, Dindshenchas</keywords>

--- Page 52 ---
<meta>This page continues the poem regarding Ochan, the burial place of the High King Niall of the Nine Hostages. Having described Niall's death and the mourning of the four provinces, the text now lists his famous sons and descendants, transitioning from the era of pagan kings to the spiritual triumph of Saint Colum Cille.</meta>

# OCHAN

The two Conalls, Eogan from the north,
<margin>50</margin>
Fiachu, Cairpre, and gentle Mane,
and Enna—who was the gathering point for the host—
while Lóegaire took his place as king. <note>These are the sons of Niall of the Nine Hostages, who founded the powerful Uí Néill dynasties. The "two Conalls" refer to Conall Gulban and Conall Cremthainne.</note>

The King who laid them beneath the silent earth—
alas for any who do not worship Him in this world!
<margin>55</margin>
He divided the Red Sea in two
out of fear for the People of God. <note>This stanza draws a parallel between the earthly power of the Irish kings and the supreme power of the Christian God, referencing the biblical Exodus.</note>

As for the children of Cairenn's son, who cross the battlefield:
even if they were all humble together,
despite their great number of warriors and horses,
<margin>60</margin>
no one could help them except the Son of God. <note>original: *Cland maic Cairne*; referring to the sons of Niall. Niall's mother was Cairenn Chasdub, a captive British princess.</note>

Dathí, a weapon-loving champion, went eastward after them;
he was strong-willed during his time.
His gathering on the journey was not weak;
he once divided the world. <note>Dathí was the legendary successor to Niall, said to have campaigned as far as the Alps, where he was killed by lightning.</note>

<margin>65</margin>
The descendant who is best above the bright-faced sun
sprang from Niall—a glorious lineage:
Colum Cille, who took possession of Iona,
the triumph of a living man who now dwells in the House of God. <note>Saint Colum Cille (Columba) was a great-great-grandson of Niall of the Nine Hostages and the founder of the monastery on the island of Iona (h-Í).</note>

---

### Variant Readings <note>original: *Apparatus Criticus*; this section notes differences in spelling and wording found across various medieval manuscripts of this poem.</note>

50. **Cairpre**: In manuscripts Y, H, S₂, and S₃, the name *Enna* appears here instead.
51. **Enna**: In manuscripts Y, H, S₂, and S₃, the name *Cairpri* appears here instead.
**gathering point**: Manuscript S reads "one-slaying."
52. **took**: Manuscript L reads "he took"; the other manuscripts read "they took."
53. **laid them**: Manuscript L reads "gave them"; the other manuscripts read "put them."
**beneath**: Manuscripts R, B, M, and S read "under the"; Y and H read "upon."
**earth**: Modernized from various abbreviations for *thalman* (the ground).
54. **alas for anyone**: Manuscript L reads "alas for those who"; others read "alas for him who."
**in**: Manuscripts R, B, Y, M, and S read "on."
55. **in two**: Manuscripts R and B read *ineo*; S reads *inteó*; M reads *aned*.
56. **out of fear**: Manuscripts B and S read "it was for fear"; R reads a corrupted form.
57. **children of the son**: Manuscript L reads "children"; all other manuscripts read "clans."
**cross the battlefield**: Manuscript L reads "battlefields are crossed"; S₂ and M read "king."
58. **if they were**: Manuscript B reads "if they used to be."
**humble**: Manuscripts L and Y use *umle*; all others use *umla*.
60. **help**: Manuscripts B, Y, and others show variations of the verb *foirred* or *forfed*.

<summary>This page concludes the poem on Ochan by listing the sons of Niall of the Nine Hostages and emphasizing that despite their earthly power, they are subject to God. It highlights Saint Colum Cille as the most glorious descendant of Niall's line.</summary>
<keywords>Niall of the Nine Hostages, Uí Néill, Lóegaire, Dathí, Colum Cille, Iona, Christian conversion, Irish genealogy, Ochan</keywords>

--- Page 53 ---
<meta>This page concludes the genealogical and legendary survey of the descendants of Niall of the Nine Hostages at Ochan. It lists Niall's sons who founded the major dynasties of medieval Ireland and concludes by celebrating Saint Colum Cille as Niall's greatest successor, marking the transition from the pagan heroic age to the Christian era.</meta>

# OCHAN

The two Conalls, Eogan in the north,
<margin>50</margin>
Fiachu, Cairpre, and gentle Mane,
and Enna—who was the gathering point for the army—
while Lóegaire became king. <note>These are the sons of Niall of the Nine Hostages. The "two Conalls" are Conall Gulban and Conall Cremthainne; Lóegaire was the High King during the time of Saint Patrick.</note>

The King who laid them beneath the silent earth—
alas for any who do not worship Him during their lifetime!
<margin>55</margin>
He divided the Red Sea into two parts
out of reverence for the People of God. <note>The poet contrasts the temporary power of Irish kings with the eternal, miraculous power of the Christian God.</note>

As for the children of Cairenn’s son, who stride across the battlefield,
to whom all men were entirely obedient:
despite the great number of young warriors and horses,
<margin>60</margin>
none could help them except the Son of God. <note>original: "Cland maic Cairne." Niall's mother was Cairenn Chasdub, said to be a daughter of a King of the Britons. His descendants are the Uí Néill.</note>

After them, a weapon-loving champion came to the East:
Dathí, who was headstrong in his time.
His gathering at the meeting place was not weak;
he divided the world in two. <note>Dathí was the legendary successor to Niall. Medieval tradition claimed he conquered parts of Europe and was killed by lightning in the Alps.</note>

<margin>65</margin>
The descendant who is the most excellent upon this bright-colored earth
of all who sprang from Niall (a splendid glory)
is Colum Cille, who established Iona—
the noblest man who ever lived, who now dwells in the House of God. <note>Saint Colum Cille (St. Columba) was a royal scion of the Uí Néill dynasty who founded the monastery of Iona in Scotland.</note>

<meta>This page transitions from the history of the descendants of Niall of the Nine Hostages to the "Dindsenchas" (lore of places) regarding Meath (Mide). It moves from the site of Ochan to the legendary origins of the central province of Ireland and the fire lit by the druid Mide.</meta>

# MEATH

->AED ÚA CARTHAIG <note>original: "Aed úa Carthaig cecinit." Aed the descendant of Carthach composed this.</note> sang:<-

Meath, the place of swift horses,
the path where Art the Solitary used to dwell; <note>Art Oen-fer was a legendary High King of Ireland and father of Cormac mac Airt.</note>
a slope full of the splendor of Lugaid, <note>Possibly Lugaid Menn, a conqueror of the region, or Lugaid mac Con.</note>
the level plain of the descendants of Conn and Cobthach. <note>Conn of the Hundred Battles and Cobthach Cóel Breg were ancestral kings of the dynasties that ruled this area.</note>

<margin>5</margin>
Why is this plain called "Meath" <note>original: "Mide"</note>
by the people of the race of Conn of the Hundred Battles?
What bright, fierce warrior—pure in his deeds—
what hero gave it this name?

Mide, the ardent son of Brath,
<margin>10</margin>
son of Death of the many hosts,
kindled a secret fire
over the descendants of Nemed of the strong hostages. <note>In Irish mythology, Nemed led the third group of people to settle in Ireland. Mide was his chief druid.</note>

For seven full years that fire remained blazing—
it was a powerful sanctuary—
<margin>15</margin>
until the fierce heat of the fire spread for a time
across the four quarters of Ireland.

Because of that fire, then
(this is no mistake or falsehood),
his successor is entitled to a perpetual payment
<margin>20</margin>
from every principal hearth in Ireland.

The noble successor of the ruler
of the joyful, bright plain of Meath is entitled to
a sack of fine-ground meal and a white pig
for every single roof-tree in Ireland. <note>This "hearth tax" reflects a legal claim by the Kings of Meath (and eventually the High Kings at Tara) to receive tribute from the other provinces of Ireland.</note>

***

<note>The following section lists various manuscript sources and their specific word variations, such as the Book of Leinster (L), the Yellow Book of Lecan (Y), and the Book of Ballymote (B).</note>

<summary>This page recounts the legendary origin of the name of Meath (Mide), attributing it to a sacred fire lit by the druid Mide, which burned for seven years and established a legal right to tribute from all of Ireland.</summary>
<keywords>Meath, Mide, Aed úa Carthaig, Art the Solitary, Conn of the Hundred Battles, Nemed, Irish mythology, Dindsenchas, hearth tax</keywords>

--- Page 55 ---
<meta>This page transitions from the history of the descendants of Niall of the Nine Hostages to the "Dindsenchas" (lore of places) regarding Meath (Mide). It moves from the site of Ochan to the legendary origins of the central province of Ireland and the fire lit by the druid Mide.</meta>

# MEATH

Meath, the land of swift horses,
the path where Art the Solitary <note>original: "Art the Solitary" or Art Oenfer, a legendary High King of Ireland and father of Cormac mac Airt.</note> used to travel;
the lowland full of the splendor of Lugaid... <note>Possibly Lugaid Menn or Lugaid mac Con, legendary figures associated with the region's conquest.</note>
the level ground belonging to the clan of Conn and Cobthach. <note>Conn of the Hundred Battles and Cobthach Cóel Breg, ancestral figures of the dynasties that ruled Meath.</note>

<margin>5</margin>
How did the plain get the name of "Meath"? <note>original: "Mide"</note>
this heritage of the descendants of Conn of the Hundred Battles?
What pure, bold descendant (a shining hero),
what warrior was it who gave it its name?

It was Mide, the fierce son of Brath,
<margin>10</margin>
the son of Deaith, a leader of armies;
for he kindled a mystical fire
over the people of Nemed, the taker of hostages. <note>In Irish mythology, Nemed led one of the early groups to settle Ireland. Mide was his chief druid.</note>

For seven full years, that fire remained blazing;
it was a reliable sanctuary and truce.
<margin>15</margin>
He spread the fierce power of that fire for a time
across the four quarters of Ireland. <note>original: "Erin"</note>

And so, it is truly because of this fire
(this is no reckless claim, nor a lie)
that their leader has a perpetual right
<margin>20</margin>
over every primary hearth in Ireland.

Thus, the right belongs to the noble heir
of the joyful and bright plain of Meath;
a portion of fine meal along with a white pig
from every household in Ireland.

***

<note>The following section lists textual variations found in different medieval manuscripts, such as the Book of Leinster (L), Rawlinson (R), and the Yellow Book of Lecan (Y).</note>

14. *was*] for in H. 15. *fire*] fire, etc. in RBYS2. *for a time*] strong, etc. in YSS2; there in M. 16. *over the four quarters*] the plain of every high king in L; under the four quarters, etc. in YM. 17. *fire*] fire in B; fire, etc. in RS2. 18. *mistake*] [various manuscript spellings of "anfót" in RBYSS2M]. *falsehood*] [various manuscript spellings of "immarba" in YMS]. 21. *has a right*] [various manuscript forms in YSS2]. 23. *meal*] roof-tree, etc. in LRBM; of meal in Y. 24. *every*] [variations "cach" or "in gach" in LYMS]. *single roof-tree*] single hearth in R; primary hearth in M.

# MEATH

<margin>25</margin>
->And then the druids of Ireland said all at once<-
->(it was no small grief to them):<-
->"This is an 'ill-smoke' <note>original: 'mí-dé', a punning etymology for the name of the province, Mide</note> brought to us in the east;<-
->it has cast an ill-shadow over our minds."<-

->Then Mide, without fail, gathered<-
<margin>30</margin>
->all the druids of Ireland into one house:<-
->he cut out their tongues—a fierce and brutal deed—<-
->from the heads of those proud and lofty druids.<-

->He buried those tongues in the earth<-
->of Uisnech in great and vast Meath,<-
<margin>35</margin>
->and he sat down upon the tongues—<-
->this chief sage and master historian.<-

->Gáine, the daughter of pure Gumór,<-
->was the foster-mother of mead-loving Mide;<-
->she was superior to all other women, though she was silent,<-
<margin>40</margin>
->she was a sage, a prophetess, and a chief druid.<-

->Gáine said with pride<-
->to Mide of the great victories:<-
->"It is 'above someone' <note>original: 'ós neoch', providing a folk etymology for the name 'Uisnech', referring to Mide sitting above the buried tongues</note> that our house was built,<-
->and because of this, the place shall be called Uisnech."<-

<margin>45</margin>
->From noble Uisnech and Mide,<-
->all of red-weaponed Ireland is measured;<-
->just as the scholar’s refined skill relates,<-
->this is the history of the place <note>original: 'dindsenchas'</note>.<-

->Protect Aéd úa Carthaig, O God,<-
<margin>50</margin>
->from the great storms of hell:<-
->May God commend his bright poem<-
->to the mead-loving King of Meath.<-

--- Page 57 ---
<meta>This page concludes the "Dindsenchas" (Lore of Places) for the province of Meath. Having described how the druid Mide lit a sacred fire, the poem now details his ruthless treatment of rival druids and provides the traditional folk etymology for the Hill of Uisnech, the symbolic center of Ireland.</meta>

# MEATH

<margin>25</margin>
And they said—and it was a cause of no small grief to them—
the druids of Ireland all together:
> "A 'bad smoke' <note>original: 'mí-dé', which the author uses as a punning origin for the name of the province, Meath/Mide</note> has been brought to us from the east;
> it has cast an ill-shadow over our minds."

Then the tireless Mide assembled
<margin>30</margin>
all the druids of Ireland into a single house,
and he cut out their tongues—a harsh omen—
from the heads of those powerful and noble druids.

He buried those tongues in the earth
of Uisnech in mighty Meath,
<margin>35</margin>
and he sat himself down over their tongues—
he, the chief prophet and master poet.

Gáine, daughter of pure Gumór,
was the foster-mother of mead-loving Mide;
she excelled all other women, though she remained silent;
<margin>40</margin>
she was a scholar, a prophetess, and a chief druid.

And Gáine spoke with a mournful cry,
standing before Mide of the great victories:
> "It is 'above someone' <note>original: 'ós neoch', a folk etymology for 'Uisnech', referring to Mide sitting above the buried tongues</note> that our house was built,
> and from this shall Uisnech be named."

<margin>45</margin>
From Uisnech and mighty Meath,
all of 'Ireland of the red weapons' is governed;
as the learned scholars relate the 'cutting' <note>original: 'snass', referring both to the cutting of the tongues and the 'shaping' of the historical account</note>,
this is how its history is derived.

O God, protect Aéd úa Carthaig <note>The poet who likely composed this version of the text</note>
<margin>50</margin>
from hell and all its storms;
may God grant His clear protection
to the mead-loving King of Meath.

<meta>This page begins the entry for Druim nDairbrech (the Ridge of Dairbrech) in the "Dindsenchas" (Lore of Places). Following the accounts of Meath and Uisnech, this poem by Fularthach shifts focus to a specific site associated with the hero Dairbre and the ancient tribes of Ireland.</meta>

# DRUIM NDAIRBRECH <note>original: "The Ridge of Dairbrech"</note>

## Fularthach sang this:

->From what is the ridge named Druim nDairbrech?<-
->Great was the day when the household earned it;<-
->By my art, you may clearly see in memory<-
->this crowded plain, like the land of Tara.<-

<margin>5</margin>->Druim nDairbrech is a beautiful fortress,<-
->a sandy wall beside a slender-sided wave;<-
->a bard’s song that serves as a foundation for a bright verse—<-
->I look out from its high and lovely peak.<-

->Smooth-faced ridge with colorful borders,<-
<margin>10</margin>->a thick-sided yoke upon its sloping side,<-
->a place like Raigne <note>An ancient plain in County Kilkenny famous in lore</note> of the gifted bards,<-
->the battle-fierce wall of red-faced Dairbre.<-

->Red-faced Dairbre, son of Lulach—<-
->he was swift to set snares after Samhain <note>The festival marking the beginning of winter (November 1st)</note>;<-
<margin>15</margin>->the son of Ligmuine with his many hosts,<-
->most ready in a fight against a foreigner <note>original: "danair," a term often applied to Viking invaders but used here for any hostile outsiders</note>.<-

->The Fidga, the Fochmand, and the Galeóin <note>Ancient tribes of Leinster traditionally associated with the Fir Bolg</note><-
->were not slow to yield him full obedience:<-
->the Fir Bolg and the countless Fir Domnann,<-
<margin>20</margin>->long-subdued was the fierce greed of their need.<-

->The tribe of the Crecraige of the raw gold,<-
->the Gúmóir and the Brecraige of the "white slaughter,"<-
->the Mendraige of song-generous Dairbre—<-
->ever-evident was the ferocity of that terrible battle.<-

<summary>This poem explains the legendary origins of Druim nDairbrech, celebrating its beauty and the authority of the red-faced hero Dairbre over the various ancient tribes of Ireland.</summary>

## DRUIM NDAIRBRECH <note>original: "The Ridge of Dairbrech"</note>

->From what does the hill of Druim Dairbrech get its name?<-
->Great was the day when the household earned the name;<-
->By my art, you may see in memory<-
->A plain as crowded as the territory of Tara.<-

->Druim Dairbrech is a beautiful fortress,<-
<margin>5</margin>->A sandy wall by the slender-sided wave;<-
->The song of a bard that will be strong in melodic verse<-
->I see, from this lovely, high peak.<-

->The smooth-browed hill with bright banks,<-
->The broad-flanked ridge with its sloping sides,<-
<margin>10</margin>->A place like Raigne <note>An ancient plain in County Kilkenny famous in lore</note> of the fortunate bards,<-
->The fiercely attacked wall of Red-faced Dairbre.<-

->Red-faced Dairbre, son of Lulach,<-
->Who was as swift as a trap <note>The original suggests a "chain-trap," likely a snare or hunting device</note> in the winter-time,<-
->The son of Ligmuine, a leader of armies,<-
<margin>15</margin>->Most ready in a savage fight.<-

->The Fidga, the Fochmaind, and the Gaileoin <note>Ancient tribes of early Ireland often associated with the Leinster region</note><-
->Were not their own masters, sooner or later;<-
->The Fir Bolg and the countless Fir Domnann <note>Legendary groups said to have inhabited Ireland before the arrival of the Tuatha Dé Danann</note>,<-
->The violence of their urgent need was forever tamed.<-
<margin>20</margin>
->The tribe of the Crecraige of the raw gold,<-
->The Gumóir and the Brecraige of the "white slaughter" <note>original: "bán-áir," likely meaning a bloodless victory or a specific type of ritualized battle</note>,<-
->The Mendraige of Dairbre, who was generous to song—<-
->The ferocity of that terrible fight was forever famous.<-

<meta>This page continues the poem regarding Druim Dairbrech (The Ridge of Dairbrech) from the Metrical Dindshenchas. It details the final defeat of the figure Dairbre by the legendary High King Tuathal Techtmar and concludes the explanation of the place-name's origin.</meta>

## DRUIM NDAIRBRECH <note>original: "The Ridge of Dairbrech"</note>

<margin>25</margin> Tuathal of the bloody warriors dealt
a headlong defeat to Red Dairbre around the swamp,
across every moorland
in the battle of Commar <note>original: "Commar," meaning a confluence of valleys or waters</note>, which was rougher than all other glens.

<margin>30</margin> Ruddy-faced Dairbre, on that hill,
parted from his troops in an unlucky hour;
Tuathal of the warlike face
bound him fast with his iron grip.

Tuathal the Wealthy, the warrior—
great was his rightful fame among princes;
<margin>35</margin> Dairbre, the subject of songs, fell
by Tuathal's axes—fearful was the slaughter <note>original: "cleaving"</note>!

Regarding this hill of the battle-line,
O swift poets, I declare:
The day I speak of is good for hearing the truth,
<margin>40</margin> to learn how the noble hill was named.

32. *with*] *before* in R.

36. *from which*] *from it* in L; *from it* in R; *with it* in M.

*noble ridge*] *ridge of smoke* in S2; *ridge of the dead* in M.

# LAGIN I. <note>original: "Leinster I," referring to the first of several poems about the naming of the province of Leinster</note>

## LAGIN I.

The royal line was destroyed around their king,
(it was an evil deed, a cause of wrath);
Labraid the Exile <note>original: "Loingsech móen," meaning "the dumb/silent exile." Legend says he was struck dumb by grief and only spoke later.</note>, with much valor,
burned Cobthach the Slender, son of Ugaine.

<margin>5</margin> The noble royal fortress, the great hill,
was called Tuaim Tenbath until that evil deed,
until Labraid, full of valor, destroyed it,
where he slaughtered his <note>Cobthach’s</note> young noblemen.

From the time he was destroyed—this is the truth—
<margin>10</margin> even Cobthach the Slender and his thirty kings,
until the great Son of Mary <note>Jesus Christ</note> was born,
was five hundred ever-pure years.

It was the beginning of strife and valor,
Cobthach’s torture of Loegaire;
<margin>15</margin> for that reason, Cobthach of the cairn fell
by the hand of the fierce grandson of Loegaire.

Labraid and three thousand Black Foreigners <note>original: "Dubgaill," likely referring to Gaulish mercenaries Labraid brought back from exile in Europe.</note>
set out on the path to the slaughter,
<margin>20</margin> in their warlike, sturdy equipment,
with their broad, grey-blue spears <note>original: "lágnib"</note>.

From those spears ever since,
the men of Leinster <note>original: "Lagin"</note> are called the "Spearmen" <note>original: "Lagin Láigne"</note>;
it was by them that Cobthach the Slender was slain,
by the hand of Labraid the Exile, despite many evils.

<margin>25</margin> It shall remain with his descendants until Doomsday,
that it was a war of very famous kings:
Ailill and Loegaire were first destroyed
by Cobthach the Slender, who struck the first blow.

---

**2.**] in L; *the royal fortress was a wretched burning*, etc. in the others. 
**3.** *Labraid*] in L. *the Exile*] omitted in L; *the one* in B. *valor*] in L; *great valor*, etc. in the others. 
**4.** *Ugaine*] spelled Augaine in L. 
**5.** *Tenbath*] in L; *Teanma* in YS; *Tenba*, etc. in the others. 
**6.** *the great hill*] in L; *the royal hill*, etc. in RBMS; *the kingly hill* in H; *the royal port (or "hill" written above)* in Y; *in the hill* in S₂.

**8.** *the young noblemen*] in L; *the warriors* in M; *the royal line*, etc. in the others. 
**9.** *since*] in LM.

### LAGIN I. <note>original: "Leinster I," referring to the first of several poems about the naming of the province of Leinster.</note>

The royal line was destroyed around their king
(it was an evil deed, a cause for wrath):
Labraid the Exile <note>original: "the Dumb Exile" (Loingsech Móen). Legend says he was struck dumb by grief after his father and grandfather were killed, only regaining his speech later.</note>, with his martial power, burned
Cobthach the Slender <note>original: "Cael"</note>, son of Ugaine.

<margin>5</margin>
Until that crime, Tuaim Tenbath was the name
of the noble royal fortress, that famous hill,
until Labraid, full of valor, sacked it,
when he slaughtered its young men.

From the day he was slain (this is the truth),
<margin>10</margin>
even Cobthach the Slender, with his thirty kings,
until the birth of the Son of Mary <note>Jesus Christ</note>
is five hundred pure years.

The beginning of struggle and strife was
the vengeance of Cobthach upon Loegaire:
<margin>15</margin>
for that reason, Cobthach of the cairn fell
by the hand of the fierce and cruel grandson of Loegaire.

Labraid and three thousand "Black Foreigners" <note>original: "Dub-Gaill," likely referring to Gaulish mercenaries Labraid brought back from exile in Europe to reclaim his kingdom.</note>
set out on the march to that slaughter,
in their warlike and sturdy battle-armor,
<margin>20</margin>
with their deep-blue spears <note>original: "lágnib"</note>.

From those spears ever since,
the men of Leinster <note>original: "Lagin"</note> were called the "Spearmen" <note>original: "Lagin Láigne"</note>;
at the hand of the Dumb Exile, amid great disaster,
by these spears Cobthach the Slender was slain.

<margin>25</margin>
This fate shall remain with his family until the Last Day <note>Judgment Day</note>:
that there will be war between kings of the same blood.
The destruction of Ailill <note>original: "Oilill"</note> and Loegaire
by the hand of Cobthach the Slender was the first killing.

### LEINSTER II. <note>original: "LAGIN II." This is the second poem in the series regarding the origins of the province name.</note>

# SLIEVE BLOOM <note>original: "SLIAB BLADMA"</note>

->Fulartach sang this<- <note>original: "Fulartach cecinit"</note>

Blod, son of Cú, son of Cas the famous,
son of Uachall of many shapes,
killed Bregmáel, the excellent smith <note>original: "gobainnd glain"</note>
of Cuirche, son of Snithe the swimmer.

<margin>5</margin> Cuirche Great-head was a bold king
over Medraige <note>a territory in modern County Galway</note> and over Herot;
it was with him that Blod, son of Cú, son of Cas the smooth-famed,
found no true protection.

<margin>10</margin> He traveled in his ship—a clear purpose—
from the mouth of the cool, clear Galway River <note>original: "Bun Gaillme"</note>,
from the Ford of Hurdles <note>original: "Áth Clíath," likely referring here to a location in the west</note> in great Herot,
to the Ford of Hurdles in Cualu <note>modern-day Dublin</note>.

<margin>15</margin> He went then, after many turnings,
to the Wood of Nár <note>original: "Ross Náir"</note>, son of Edlicon,
until he took possession of a specific portion there—
that mountain whose namesake is Blod.

<margin>20</margin> There died <unclear>      </unclear> the fair, who used to weave valor,
in Slieve Bloom, of vast fame—
Blad, son of Bregon, with hosts of lords—
of a plague in the monster-haunted mountain of Blod.

<margin>Alternatively, Or</margin> <note>original: "Aliter Nó"</note> It is from the son of Bregon the proud
that Slieve Bloom with its womanly-breasted peaks <note>original: "mban-tolcaib"</note> is named;
its profit is not far from hardship—
the mountain as it came to be from mighty Blad.

---

## SLIEVE BLOOM <note>original: "SLIAB BLADMA"</note>

Blod, son of Cú, son of Cas the renowned,
son of Uachall of many shapes,
killed Bregmáel the famous smith
of Cuirche, son of Snithe the swimmer.

Cuirche Great-head <note>original: "Cendmar"</note> was a daring king <margin>5</margin>
over Medraige and over Herot;
because of him, Blod, son of Cas Clothmin,
never found sure protection.

He traveled in his ship—a clear purpose!—
from the Bottom <note>or mouth</note> of pure-cold Galway, <margin>10</margin>
from the Ford of Hurdles <note>Áth Cliath</note> in wide Herot
to the Ford of Hurdles in Cualu <note>modern-day Dublin</note>.

From there he came, after many a turn,
to the Point of Nár, son of Edliuc,
and possessed, as his own special portion, <margin>15</margin>
the mountain whose name derives from Blod.

---

A valiant man who used to wage battle died
at Slieve Bloom—vast renown!—
even Blad, son of Bregon, with troops of warriors,
died of disease in the monster-haunted mountain of Blod. <margin>20</margin>

Or, it is from the son of Bregon the wrathful
that it is named Slieve Bloom, with the onsets of women;
their profit (?) not far removed from destruction (?)
was the mountain where it happened through strong Blad.

---

### FID NGABLI <note>The Wood of Gabol</note>

->Finn sang this:<-

Dear to me is the bright Gabol,
which brought a burden to the bright-stemmed wood;
not for the sake of a price that would fade,
did he ask for it to be named after him.

<margin>5</margin>Ainge gathered a bright bundle <note>original: grinne</note>,
without concealment as it dripped;
every tree was searched without fear,
to be included in the soft, fresh-topped bundle.

He made a tub <note>original: drochta</note> for his daughter,
<margin>10</margin>above the work of the high river-mouth’s breast;
it would not leak unless the sea were full:
he cherished the condition of her maidenhood.

It was he who stole it—as the story goes—
Gaible the pale, son of Ethedeon;
<margin>15</margin>he cast it away without a reward for his toil,
from the cold Pass of Fualascach <note>original: Beluch Fúar Fhualascaig</note>.

He took up residence among the tribes of Flann;
his thicket and his true forest belong to him,
the man who stole and carried it off in the east;
<margin>20</margin>among women, he was always beloved.

---

**LRBYMEHSS₃.** *Finn]* RB only. 1. *to me]* to us R; hand E. *Gabol]* Gabrán, etc. ES. 2. *brought a burden]* brought a burden to me L; he frequented R; he used to frequent M; he frequented, etc. the rest. *the wood]* upon the L (but *at the wood* at 159 b 7). *bright-stemmed]* leafy LRBHS₃. 3. *not]* not for R. *sake]* cause, etc. LBM; cause R; good HS₃; gæ (with *d* superscript) Y; ghái, etc. SE. *price]* etc. LM; omitted R; price, etc. the rest. *with]* without, etc. RYS; though E. *decay]* movement E; movement S. 4. *he asked]* he will go LB; he asked Y; he gave M; he took H; he took R; so that he took S₃. *from him his]* from him LS; from him RYHS₃; from her M. 5. *gathered]* increased M. *Ainge]* Aine RE; the spear M. *bundle]* etc. LM; the bundle, etc. the rest. *bright]* LHS₃; bright the rest. 6. *concealment]* stealth L; fear (in an erasure) S; fear E; concealment M. *during]* toward H; through SE; through M.

<summary>This poem, attributed to Finn Mac Cumaill, explains the naming of Fid Ngabli after Gaible, son of Ethedeon, who stole a magical bundle of wood used to make a leak-proof tub for Ainge's daughter. Gaible cast the bundle away at the Pass of Fualascach, where the wood eventually grew and took his name.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the poetic history of Fid Ngabli (the Wood of Gabol), providing a translation of the verses describing the gathering and theft of a sacred bundle of wood that gave the location its name.</meta>

<page-num>59</page-num>
<header>( 59 )</header>

### FID NGABLI.

Dear to me is bright Gabul
who set the bright-stemmed wood in motion;
not for the sake of a reward that would decay,
he prayed that it should be named after him.

Ainge gathered a bright bundle
which dripped unless it was low tide:
every kind of tree without exception was sought
in the soft, fresh-leaved bundle.

A tub was made for his daughter
<margin>10</margin>above the breastwork of the high river mouth;
it would not leak unless the tide was full:
she cherished the state of virginity.

He was the one who stole it (the theme of a story),
even Gaible the pale, son of Ethedeon;
<margin>15</margin>he threw it without payment for his labor
from the cold Pass of the Thicket.

It found rest in the borders of Fland;
he rightfully claims his grove and his own wood,
the man who thieved and stole in the east;
<margin>20</margin>he was dear to women at all times.

---

# MAG LIFE.

Bright Life, of ample fame,
daughter of Cannan of the hundred coracles,
gained a title of renown as a reward for her journey;
the name of the plain is a great gift.

The cup-bearer of prudent Conaire
brought a choice, fine gift
to the daughter of Cannan of the hundred hides:
Deltbanna of the white teeth and bright face.

Childbirth was the ruin of eager Life
at Port Agmar in Aran:
the son of Drucht died because of it,
from the great sorrow for heroic Life.

## BERBA.

The Barrow, enduring in its silence,
travels through the host of ancient Ailbe;
it is knowledge of its power that is the reason for the name
Barrow, the flower of every word of praise. <margin>4</margin>

It would not have seethed had there not been cast into it <margin>5</margin>
the ashes of Mechi, the great destroyer;
the bitter filth of that ancient serpent
made it silent and boiling, without respite.

A serpent that coiled itself three times,
haunting the stronghold to devour it; <margin>10</margin>
by its nature, it would have consumed every herd
and the peaceful people of ancient Ireland.

For 그 reason Diancecht killed it;
it was a harsh cause for a noble deed,
to stop it forever from its devouring <margin>15</margin>
and from consuming every harbor.

I know its grave, where it was cast—
a tomb without house or roof;
its evil ashes, without beauty or mark,
found stillness in the noble Barrow. <margin>20</margin>

---

<meta>This page continues the legend of the River Berba (the Barrow) from the Metrical Dindshenchas, providing a version of the poem regarding the serpent slain by the god Diancecht and the origin of the river's name.</meta>

# BERBA.

The Barrow, enduring in its silence,
which flows through the people of ancient Ailbe;
it is a labor to learn the reason why it is called
the smooth Barrow, the finest of all famous names.

No motion was caused in it 5
by the ashes of Mechi, who was severely struck;
the stream made the deadly filth of the ancient serpent
soaked and silent, beyond recovery.

The serpent made three turns;
it sought out the soldier to devour him; 10
by its actions, it would have destroyed all the cattle
of the peaceful hosts of ancient Ireland.

Therefore, Diancecht <note>The physician-god of the Tuatha Dé Danann</note> slew it;
there was a harsh reason for destroying it completely,
to prevent it forever from causing destruction 15
worse than any wolf-pack, and from devouring everything.

I know its grave where he threw it,
a tomb without walls or a roof-beam;
its ashes, evil and without the beauty of innocence,
found a silent burial in the noble Barrow. 20

### MOIN GAI GLAIS.

Culdub, son of Dian, on the day of Samhain,
went far away—a famous battle—
to seek single combat
with generous, most joyful Fidrad.

<margin>5</margin>From that encounter, Fidrad fell
by the clash of the strong enemies:
the death of keen Fidrad after his wrath,
by the hand of the son of Dian of the red flame.

<margin>10</margin>Gae Glass, grandson of Lugh of the graves,
was handsome, resisting the flow of blood:
he possessed a spear tempered against floods,
which Aith, the noble smith, had fashioned.

He was the champion of generous Fiachu,
grandson of Lugh, the fierce and sharp:
<margin>15</margin>he was a warrior in danger without payment for his passing:
by his hand, Culdub fell.

# FAFFAND.

->Fulartach (?) sang.<-

Powerful Broccaid of the deed of hostages,
of the bright, noble race of the Galians;
his son was Faifne the poet—
the memory of his final madness is no lie.

<margin>5</margin>The mother of that handsome son was
Libir of the fierce, swift presence;
their daughter was the swift leader of troops,
the noble Aige of many deeds.

<margin>10</margin>Very handsome was that fair, curly-haired quartet;
they were a noble family of good character;
father and mother with beauty,
daughter and brother, smooth and fair.

The spirits of the mounds brought it about—
it was no faint deed of wild frenzy—
<margin>15</margin>they transformed into the shape of a wild fawn
the noble Aige with lovely spots.

She traversed Ireland from coast to coast,
fleeing before every fierce and fiery pack of hounds,
until she circled Banba of the judgments,
<margin>20</margin>bravely through its four quarters.

Her deed and her valor came to an end;
here her dispersal was forever found;
the war-bands of Meilge of Imlech
tore her into pieces in their malice.

### FAFFAND.

Powerful Broccaid with the taking of hostages,
of the bright and famous race of the Galians,
he had a son, Faifne the poet;
the record of his final journey is no lie.

<margin>5</margin>She was the mother of the handsome son—
even Libir, quick and eager of spirit:
their daughter was the swift lady of the hosts,
Aige, the noble and skillful.

Exceedingly fair were the four, curly-haired and gentle;
<margin>10</margin>they were a noble family, of virtuous behavior:
the father and the lovely mother,
the daughter and the brother, gentle and fair.

The evil spirits made an attack
(it was no weak deed of reckless folly):
<margin>15</margin>they changed into the form of a wild doe
the noble Aige of the beauty-spots.

She traversed Ireland from shore to shore,
fleeing before all the fierce and fiery packs;
so that she ran around Banba <note>A poetic name for Ireland</note>, land of judges,
<margin>20</margin>bravely, four full times.

Her deeds and her bravery came to an end,
here her final destruction occurred;
they tore her into pieces in their wickedness—
the warriors of Meilge of Imlech did this.

### FAFFAND.

<margin>25</margin>From that comes the cold Aige
upon the stream of the plain, great of beauty;
since she was tormented without secrecy,
she was scattered upon the swift water.

That is an ancient pool without death forever,
<margin>30</margin>which flows over the Liffey in its fierce way;
a work of contempt—no sound without a house—
Aige is its name at all times.

Westward in his haste went
the swift druid, the noble poet,
<margin>35</margin>to satirize the famous king of Beirre,
Meilge, the son of prudent Cobthach.

He rightfully proclaimed against the king
both blemish and shame at once,
and disgrace with long-lasting fury of spirit,
<margin>40</margin>in revenge for his noble sister.

The keen poet fell
because of that fierce and terrible cause;
he was betrayed for a long time
for satirizing the king of great Tara.

<margin>45</margin>He was punished and mangled,
he was parted from his joy;
on Faffand of the angry war-bands,
he met a swift and marauding chase.

### FAFFAND.

<margin>25</margin>Hence is the name of cold Aige
given to the river of the many-colored plain,
since she was tortured without secrecy
and flung upon the flowing water.

That ancient stream is deathless until Doomsday,
<margin>30</margin>which pours across the Liffey in a furious way:
(a deed of shame, not wrongly rumored?)—
Aige is its name for all time.

for satirizing the king of high Tara.

he was parted from his misery;
in Faffand of the wrathful warriors,
he met the pursuit of swift marauders.

### FAFFAND.

He entreated for a mutual hacking there,
at the place where the warrior was cut down; <margin>50</margin>
so that—a deed of lamentation—
the ancient mound might bear his name forever.

I know well, indeed, through mockery,
the death of Libir and of Broccaid;
not obscure is the reason for the existence <margin>55</margin>
of the rampart in which Broccaid was buried.

### ALMU I.

Almu of Leinster, the court of the Fianna,
the place where truly generous Finn used to dwell;
here occurs that particular tradition
of why Almu is named Almu.

<margin>5</margin>
Almu was the name of the man who held it
in the time of Nemed of mighty fame;
he died there on the green hill,
of a sudden sickness all at once.

Almu, the woman, was beautiful—
<margin>10</margin>
the wife of great Nuada son of Achi;
she requested—it was a true agreement—
her name for the whole hill.

Nuada the druid was a fierce man;
by him was built a firm, high fort;
<margin>15</margin>
alum was rubbed by him on the wall
of the whole fort after its marking out.

The fort was uniquely white—a fierce struggle—
as if it had received the brightness of Ireland;
from the alum he brought to his house,
<margin>20</margin>
from that is Almu named Almu.

Tadc son of Nuada, who strengthens valor,
the druid of Cathair the Great with much fame,
to him his father left
Almu with its high ramparts.

<margin>25</margin>
A lovely daughter had strong Tadc,
whose name was Muirne of the fair neck;
the woman was sought by Cumall;
white-sided Tadc refused him.

---

*L only, except stanzas 3, 5, 21, which are quoted in LU; see notes.* <margin>17. *dremne]*</margin>

### ALMU I.

Almu of the Leinstermen, a fort of the Fianna,
a dwelling that Finn the truly noble used to frequent:
here came by chance one of no common lineage,
the woman for whom Almu is so named.

<margin>5</margin>Almu is the name of the man who obtained the place
in the time of Nemed of mighty renown;
he died there on the green hill
of a sudden sickness in a moment.

Almu—beautiful was the woman!
<margin>10</margin>the wife of Great Nuada, son of Achi;
she requested—it was a fair decision—
that her name should be on the entire hill.

Nuada the druid was a fierce man;
by him a strong and high fort was built:
<margin>15</margin>alum was rubbed by him on the rock
over the whole fort, after it was marked out.

The fort is all white (bitter strife),
as if it had received the lime of all Ireland,
from the alum he put on his house,
<margin>20</margin>from that Almu is so named.

Tadc, son of Nuada, who strengthens valor,
the druid of Cathair the Great, famous in renown,
to him his father left
Almu with her noble possessions.

<margin>25</margin>Strong Tadc had a lovely daughter
whose name was Muirne of the Fair Neck;
the woman was sought by Cumhall;
Tadc the fair-sided refused him.

# ALMU I.

Cumhall took her by force,
<margin>30</margin>the daughter of Tadc, though it was a hateful deed;
for a year without right or submission,
Muirne was held by battle-mighty Cumhall.

Tadc wept strongly for a space of time
<margin>35</margin>before brave Conn of the Hundred Battles;
he reproached him—the evil was vast—
he satirized him; it was a great injury.

It was commanded by brave Conn
that Cumhall must leave Banba;
<margin>40</margin>they fought the battle of Cnucha yonder,
and Cumhall fell by Conn’s hand.

Nine <term>tráths</term> before the battle was fought,
the man of grace was begotten
<margin>45</margin>upon the daughter of white-sided Tadc;
thus was Finn, the true warrior, conceived.

Muirne went after the killing of her husband
until she reached all-white Almu;
she was plaintive and sorrowful;
<margin>50</margin>it was not fitting for the noble woman.

The queenly woman was beautiful and gracious,
and she was heavy with child;
Tadc commanded—a great deed—
<margin>55</margin>her killing and her destruction.

Conn of the white hands said:
"I would prefer that she bear a son;
the mother of grace is the same
for Cumhall and for my father."

<margin>60</margin>Tadc refused [this] strongly;
it was a cause of tears for Muirne;
<note>nevertheless</note> they did not dare to speak to her,
to ruin her, or to kill her prematurely.

# ALMU I.

Cumall carried her off by force,
<margin>30</margin>the daughter of Tadc, though it was a hateful deed;
for a year, without right and without victory,
the warrior Cumall possessed Muirne.

Tadc wept bitterly for a time
<margin>35</margin>before brave Conn of the Hundred Battles:
he taunted him—the evil was enormous!—
he reviled him—the injury was great!

A sentence was given by brave Conn
against Cumall, that he should leave Banba;
so they fought the battle of Cnucha there,
<margin>40</margin>and Cumall fell before Conn.

Nine hours before the battle was fought,
the Man of Luck was conceived;
upon the daughter of white-sided Tadc,
Finn, the true warrior, was conceived.

<margin>45</margin>Muirne arrived after the slaying of her husband,
and traveled to all-white Almu;
she was plaintive and sorrowful,
which was not fitting for the high-born lady.

The princess was lovely and gracious,
<margin>50</margin>and she was heavy with child;
Tadc threatened (a terrible deed!)
to kill her and make an end of her.

White-palmed Conn said:
“I consider it better that she should bear a son;
<margin>55</margin>the same mother, with her heritage,
was shared by Cumall and my father.”

She was vehemently rejected by Tadc,
(which was a cause of tears to Muirne)
yet he did not dare to do what he spoke of—
<margin>60</margin>to destroy them or kill them suddenly.

# ALMU I.

Fair-necked Muirne went
to fair Tara of Fail . . . .
and she asked Conn, the sinless,
about her circumstances and her destiny.

<margin>65</margin> “Go,” said Conn, “with my permission,
to Fiacail Fí son of Conchenn;
Cumall’s sister is there within,
Bodmall B . . . .”

Fair-necked Muirne went
<margin>70</margin> to Tara of Margi with dignity;
Conla, the sinless servant of Conn,
[went] . . . . to arrange her affairs.

The pleasant youth was joyful at her arrival,
that is Fiacail Fí son of Conchenn;
<margin>75</margin> Bodmall was certainly joyful,
and the whole assembly was joyful.

After that, excellent Finn was born,
king of the Fianna, great in spirit;
nine years . . . . a firm way,
<margin>80</margin> he was the royal champion of Ireland.

Finn demanded of Tadc of the towers
compensation for the killing of great Cumall:
a battle without respite or delay,
or to be granted single combat.

<margin>85</margin> Because Tadc was not capable of battle
against the true prince,
he surrendered to him—it was enough for him—
just as all of Almu stood. A.

### ALMU II.

Almu was lovely of foot,
daughter of Beccán of the bright cloak,
wife of Iuchna of the tresses, with a hundred cattle,
from whom Almu sought its name.

Great for her virtues and her bands, <margin>5</margin>
for her hosts and her ancient halls,
her fame was known melodiously,
from which the great Almu is named.

When the Fianna of the son of Cumall of noble honor <margin>10</margin>
used to gather for a meeting,
you were a seat for fierce men with spears;
noble and high was Almu.

When the Clans of Bresail of the long-lasting battle
came to her noble couch <margin>15</margin>
on a journey of pleasure across the beauty of Segais,
the good Almu was *almain* <note>nourishment</note> to them.

### ALMU II.

Almu was fair of foot,
the daughter of Beccán of the bright robe,
the wife of Iuchna of the tresses, with a hundred head of cattle,
from whom Almu sought its name.

its fame was known in song,
from which it is called mighty Almu.

came to her splendid feast,
<margin>15</margin>with desire for good cheer, across . . . . of Segais,
noble Almu was *nourishment* for them.

# ALEND.

Alend, an assembly place for our warriors,
the fort of Art with its royal roads;
a chariot-track of victory on its plain,
until Fál son of Fidga found it.

<margin>5</margin> The grave where Conchend the champion settled,
the son of Fergna, a man of bright beauty;
the pilot of the plain of Lugaid, hacker of white shields,
at the dwelling of Setna of the long bow.

A land where stern Messdelmond used to be;
<margin>10</margin> by him its high wall was built.
It was from its springs that he drank a draught,
Mess Scott Scegra of the Leinstermen.

The green of Ruamand, reddening of spear-points,
with the enclosures of a very fair prince;
<margin>15</margin> a lovely land, a perfect fortress,
a sun-chamber where Andrithir used to stay.

The territory of Fergus of the Sea,
a noble inheritance of renown;
the domain of Find son of Ross of the feats,
<margin>20</margin> the royal height of Bresal Bregaman.

Luchdond, hacking cheeks—a cause of grief—
from Gabrán to Dublin,
at the Wall of Segais the waters flowed;
fierce were his deeds around Alend. ->A.<-

## ALEND.

Alend, a gathering place for our warriors,
the fort of Art with his royal roads:
he was a chariot-track of victory on its plain
until Fal, son of Fidga, encountered him:

<margin>5</margin> The grave where Conchend the champion settled,
the son of Fergna, a hero of fair fame,
field-captain of Lugaid, hewer of shields;
over the seat of Setna Long-staff:

The place where stern Messdelmond lived,
<margin>10</margin> by him its lofty wall was built;
from its springs a draught was drunk
by Mess Scegra the Scot of Leinster:

The lawn of Ruamand, where the spear-point grew red,
with the enclosures of the honorable prince;
<margin>15</margin> a lovely land, a perfect citadel,
the sun-chamber where Andrithir lived:

The domain of Fergus Fairge,
a proud and eminent heritage:
the portion of nimble Find son of Ross,
<margin>20</margin> the royal keep of Bressal Bregaman:

Luchdond, grievous scarring of cheeks,
from Gabrán even to Dublin!
at the Wall of Segais he would swim the water,
dire were his deeds around Alend:

<meta>This page continues the poetic history of Alend (the Hill of Allen) from the Dindsenchas, detailing its association with legendary kings like Catháir Már and Fiachu, and its geographical connection to the river Liffey.</meta>

## ALEND.

A bear of many spears, a flame of courage, <margin>25</margin>
a vigorous resting-place for warriors;
in the time of Nia Corb, the brave chief,
Alend was a skillful dwelling: A.

The chariot of Catháir, a casket of treasures, <margin>30</margin>
stoutly it encompassed the lords of the herds;
the talk of every gathering, a bright deed,
high-king of Emain and Alend: A.

The board-game of Fiachu, victorious king,
a fiery dragon, firm of body, <margin>35</margin>
he reddened spear-points through kings,
he bound the heroes of Alend: A.

The hill of Bressal the loud-mouthed in valor:
by him Tuaim Tenbad was Tara;
a noble king on a fine horse <margin>40</margin>
who brandished the weapons of Alend: A.

A lordly stream reaches it,
the Segais that flows from Sid Nechtain;
the Liffey, swiftest of its waters:
they laugh upon the flat plain of Alend. <margin>45</margin>

Three strong men, builders of trenches,
Buirech, Fiach, and Aururas,
they were without failure, a bright task,
they dug the rampart of Alend. A.

# ALEND.

<margin>25</margin>A furious (?) bear, a flame of valor,
a resting-place giving vigor to heroes;
in the time of Nia Corb (a brave chief!),
you were a home for the wise, O Alend!

The chariot of Cathair, a casket of treasures;
<margin>30</margin>valiantly did he surround the leaders of the herds;
the subject of all conversation (a clear fact!)
is the high king of Emain and Alend.

The chessboard of Fiachu, the victorious king,
a fiery dragon (stout of body!);
<margin>35</margin>through his kings he made spear-points red;
he bound the champions of Alend.

The hill of Bressal Beolach the valorous—
to him belonged Tuaim Tenbath Temair—
against elegant horses, the famous king
<margin>40</margin>brandished the weapons of Alend.

A lordly river visits it:
the Segais which flows from Sid Nechtain,
and the Liffey, swiftest of its waters;
they drain the bare plain of Alend.

<margin>45</margin>Three mighty men made attempts at trenching:
Burech, Fiach, and Aururas.
It is they who, without flagging (a clear fact!),
dug the rampart of Alend.

---

níthu, etc. BYMSE; niothe S₃. 37. *béolaig*] belaig, etc. ME. *fri gail*] ri gail L; with renown, etc. others. 38. *Tenbad*] Tenba RES₃; tenfa, etc. YM; tenbai H. 40. *armu*] weapons YSS₃H; exploit M. 41. *sæglond*] noble, etc. YSE. *nodassaig*] it approaches them L (with *or approaches* written above); it approaches them (with *d* written above) B; it approaches them R; it approaches them E. 42. *snás*] a flow L. 43. *Life*] Liphea L; is Liffey S. *luathem linne*] L; swift around the pool, etc. BS; swift around the pool, etc. RHS₃; swift about the pool Y; swift in the pool E; swift around the beautiful pool M. 44. *tibit tulmag*] L; against the choice of the people RB; against the choice of the terrifying people, etc. YSS₃H; against the choice of the tribes E; against the choice of the tribes M. 45. *trialla*] attempts BRHS₃; attempts a SE; trials a M; for attempts a Y. 46. *Buirech Fiach is*] Fiach and Buirech L. 47. *monor*] exploit SE. *ngle*] omitted R. 48. *acclaidset*] they dug M; they dug, etc. SE; they dug S₃.

# NOTES.

## RATH ESA.

ETAIN (or Etaine) was a princess of the *áes side* <note>the fairy folk</note>, among whom she lived for a thousand years as the wife of Midir, until through the jealousy and magic arts of her rival Fuamnach, she was carried off from her fairy husband and underwent a second birth as the daughter of a mortal. She became the wife of Eochaid Airem, King of Ireland; but Midir won her back from him at chess and carried her, along with her daughter Esa, to his fairy palace of Bri Leith. This poem tells how Eochaid won back his wife and daughter. The sequel to the fortunes of Etain, Midir, and Eochaid is contained in the *Bruden Da Derga*. For the literature on the subject, see the introduction to Stokes’ edition of that tale and to Windisch’s edition of the *Tochmarc Etáine* (*Irische Texte* 1, 113). A translation of the LU <note>Lebor na hUidre</note> text of the *Tochmarc* will be found in Thurneysen’s *Sagen aus dem alten Irland*, 77. The whole series of legends, and the different versions of each, have been fully discussed by Nettlau in volumes 12 and 13 of the *Revue Celtique*.

Midir's abduction of Etain is referred to again in the Dindshenchas article on Rath Cruachan.

This poem is ascribed in manuscript S₂ to Cinaeth úa Hartacán: in manuscript H to “Maicniadh mac Aonaccan.”

2. As *tichtain* is feminine, we must either read *bruthmair* against the manuscripts, or regard *bruthmar* as a nominative agreeing with *báire*.

5. *Esa* is also called Etain: see IT i. 130, 15.

7. The prose Dindshenchas says that ‘a hundred of every kind of cattle were brought [by Esa] to Midir, after Etain had been carried off from Eochaid.’

8. *bés* seems here to mean ‘tax, tribute’ (see Meyer, *Contributions*), and to refer to the cattle gathered by Esa.

LL <note>Book of Leinster</note> reads *búan* for *buaid*; and this may be right, but I have no other instance of this word used as a substantive.

12. The gloss on this poem printed by Meyer in ACL iii. 143 from manuscript H. 3. 18, page 467, explains *nen* by *cumal*; and this is supported by LBr 238<sup>d</sup> a 24 where a *nen* is glossed as a *hinilt*. It is also possible that we should read *n-ena*: this adjective, which means perhaps ‘proud,’ occurs in SnR 4431 *immon n-eclais n-ena n-aird*; LL 192 *b* 35 *comul n-ena*; BB 374 *a* 32 *conid and sid n-ena* (a proper name?); BB 17 *b* 7 *reim n-eana*.

<summary>This page provides historical and philological commentary on the poem "Rath Esa," summarizing the legend of Etain and Midir and analyzing specific manuscript variants.</summary>
<keywords>Etain, Midir, Eochaid Airem, Rath Esa, Dindshenchas, Bri Leith, Irish mythology, Tochmarc Etáine, philology, manuscripts</keywords>

--- Page 102 ---
<meta>This page continues the philological notes for the poem "Rath Esa," providing commentary on specific lines and linguistic forms found in the manuscripts.</meta>

<language>English</language>
<page-type>text</page-type>
<page-num>90</page-num>
<header>NOTES. [RATH ESA</header>

14. *ólaig*. It was at a banquet that Midir appeared and carried off Etain. He promises her: *Cid mesc lib coirm Inse Fáil, is mescu coirm tíre máir* (LU 131 *b* 35).

15. *aire-glan*. This epithet is repeated in line 23, and in line 89 it is applied to Etain. It seems to be derived from *aire*, meaning "the temples." As applied to a place, it can only be understood metaphorically; *grúaid* ("cheek") is similarly used of places; for example, LL 164 *b* 19, for *gruaid Ailig*; 215 *a* 38 *dar slim-gruad sær sen-Charmuin*. But in line 15 it may refer to Etain, and perhaps it found its way from there to line 23 through the error of an early scribe. The variant *airerglan* is glossed by O'Clery as *oirearglan .i. aoibhinn-glan* <note>delightfully pure</note>.

27. *dolaim* is supported by the rhyme. I do not know the word as a noun, but I suppose it to be connected with *dolam* ("slow") or *dolma* ("delay") in the *Saltair na Rann*. However, the true reading may be *oca ndlomaisin*, meaning "repulsing them": compare O'Clery's *dlomaisin*.

32. *ruibnech*. Manuscript H. 3. 18 (as mentioned above) explains this word as *robuidnech*: compare Windisch's dictionary under *ruibne (2)*. But this meaning does not suit *Bend Etair* ii. 66 (Todd Lectures vii. 60) *cend ruad ruibnech*; compare the index of *Cath Finntrágha* under the same word.

41. *Mess Buachalla*: see *Bruden Da Derga* (edited by Stokes), pages 11 and 12.

42, 44. *chélig—Ébir*. Manuscript L has *chelig*, without the accent mark: the other readings provide no rhyme. But what does *chélig* mean? At *Mag Life* 6 (below), L again reads *Conaire chelig* against the other <term>MSS</term>: at *Ráth Chnamrossa* 10 (LL 195 *a* 34) we have *mac Conaire chélig*: for *célech*, see Meyer's *Contributions*. The objection to reading *chélig* in our passage is that *Eber* generally has no accent mark. But this is by no means always the case: see LL 14 *a* z, 14 *b* 18, 15 *a* 3, 21, 16 *b* 2, 37, 42. It seems, then, that the vowel length is undetermined, and I feel justified in writing *Ébir*.

43. *fuachalda*: the reading and meaning are uncertain. O'Clery has *nuacholla .i. uathbhásach* <note>terrible</note>.

Midir induced Eochaid to commit unwitting incest with his daughter; Mess Buachalla was the offspring of this unnatural union: see *Revue Celtique* xii. 235-239. Line 43 seems to refer to this.

46. *Brí Léith*. Manuscript L has *bria*. The true genitive is *breg*: the dative is *brig*: in lines 12 and 24 all <term>MSS</term> have *brí*.

55. *decharda* may be referred to *dechur* ("division"; so Meyer, ZCP iii. 429), or to *dechrad* ("fury").

57. The fragmentary tale in LU 131 gives an account of the making of the *tochur* <note>causeway</note>. Eochaid learned from the *Síde* <note>fairy folk</note>, on this occasion, the art of making oxen work in a yoke; hence his nickname *Airem*, "Ploughman." *Iarsin doberar uir ocus grian ocus clocha forsin monai. For etnu dam dano bátár fedmand la firu h-Erend cosin n-aidchi-sin, conaicces la lucht in t-shide for a formnaib. Do gníth samlaid la Eochaidg, conid de atá do-som Echaid Airem, ar is aice toisech tucad cuing for muinélaib dam do feraib h-Erend.*

<vocab>Midir, Etain, Eochaid Airem, Mess Buachalla, Brí Léith, tochur, Síde, LU, LL, SnR, O'Cl.</vocab>

<meta>This page continues the philological notes for the poem "Rath Esa," specifically discussing lines 67 through 94 and providing context for the figures and locations mentioned in the text.</meta>

<page-num>91</page-num>
# RATH ESA] NOTES.

It seems from this that the four things demanded are corvées <note>unpaid labor</note> imposed on Midir's people rather than rights claimed by Eochaid, as Stokes suggests, <term>RC</term> xvi. 308. Compare <term>LU</term> 132 a 5.

67. -*asoerad* seems to be the imperfect subjunctive of *asorgim*, for -*esorrad* (?).

69. These three fortresses are named together in a verse written on the top margin of <term>LL</term> 164 (quoted in *Silva Gadelica* ii. 520). *belgach* seems to be a derivative of *belach*; at all events, it must be distinct from *bélgach*, meaning "talkative."

70. *cétach* is a bad rhyme for *Étar*; and the disagreement among the manuscripts indicates corruption.

77. The reading of manuscript L, *coll* for *cúl*, is perhaps correct: "after a breach of the truce."

81. *bile*, like *gas*, etc., is used metaphorically for persons. See Meyer, *Contributions*; and compare *Temair* iv. 14 *ba borg-bile co mbadb-slait* (Todd Lectures viii. 28).

89-96. The events referred to here are known only through casual allusions. In <term>LL</term> 121 b 52, these lines occur:—

->*Atá cend Echdach indiu*<-
->*is-Síd Nenta iar n-usciu.*<-

It seems that Sigmall carried on the quarrel of his grandfather Midir and finally destroyed Eochaid Airem at Fremand: see <term>LL</term> 23 a 37, 131 b 39; <term>FM</term> 5084. His own fate is recorded in <term>LL</term> 11 b 20 (compare <term>LU</term> 129 b 18). O’Donovan identifies his palace of Síd Nenta (in his note on <term>FM</term> 5084) with Mullaghshee in Roscommon. In his *Hy Many*, page 6, he had placed it at Fairymount near the north end of Lough Ree. The words *iar n-usciu* need not be regarded as a note of a real place: such dwellings of the *áes síde* <note>fairy folk</note> were often imagined as opening on "seas in fairy lands forlorn": compare Nutt, *Voyage of Bran*, i. 229. In the *Bruden Da Chocae*, this place is again called *Sith Nenta ier n-usce* (<term>RC</term> 21, 152); while in the *Cophur in da Muccida*, it is referred to as *Síd Nento fo huisce* (<term>IT</term> iii. 238 (line 90), 240 (line 280)). *Argain Síde Nenta* is the title of one of the lost tales: see O'Curry, *Lectures*, 591.

85. *Ogniad* is mentioned in the catalogue of women, <term>LL</term> 137 a 30.

94. *síd*: perhaps meaning "peace."

<keywords>Midir, Eochaid Airem, Síd Nenta, Sigmall, Irish mythology, philology, place names, Irish manuscripts, LL, LU, O'Donovan</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the philological notes for the poems in the Dindshenchas, moving from "Rath Esa" on the previous page to "Brug I," which focuses on the famous archaeological and mythological site of Newgrange.</meta>

<page-num>92</page-num>
<header>NOTES. [BRUG I.</header>

## BRUG I.

The *Brug mic ind Óc* is the famous *Brug na Bóinde* <note>Newgrange</note>. The story of the trick by which the Mac ind Oc obtained it from his father the Dagda is told in the Book of Leinster 209 *a* 50, and following.

1. *a maig*. All manuscripts except S agree here on this form of the vocative, whereas in line 74 of the Leabhar na hUidhre (the only manuscript containing it) has *a mag*.

2. The reading of all the later manuscripts, *srethaib sét*, would mean ‘with a series of roads’; compare *Ailech* ii. 31 (Todd Lecture Series vii. 44) *dar sreith slige*.

3. *forolgais*; derived from *folaigim*.

4. *immotrét* seems to be a t-preterite, from \*imm-emaim.

7. *féig*. Compare *Irische Texte* iii. 535 *escra féig*, where Windisch translates it as ‘clear.’

8. *indsech* possibly means ‘peaceful’: compare *Irische Texte* ii. 1, 41, line 1280, *bith-indes* (so read). However, *indsech* meaning ‘islanded’ occurs in *Togail Trói* <note>The Destruction of Troy</note>.

13. *Ingen Foraind*: presumably the daughter of Pharaoh and wife of *Miled* is meant: see O’Mahony’s edition of Keating, page 164.

14. *mál*: as a rule, this term is not applied to a woman; but see O’Clery’s Glossary under the word.

16. The reading is doubtful. The form *ignod* in the Leabhar na hUidhre is difficult to explain. Perhaps it should be *in gnod*. This would supply alliteration and a response to *tor* in line 15; but the meaning would be obscure: *gnod* is glossed as *rinn* <note>a point or star</note>: see Stokes, *Metrical Glossaries*, pages 22 and 85.

17. ‘Fiacc’s Pool’ is mentioned as being ‘on the Boyne,’ in *Irische Texte* iv., lines 56 and 2346: compare the Book of Leinster 209 *b* 42 *Féic lind*.

25. For the *Mátha* (or *Máta*) <note>a legendary monster</note>, see the *Dindshenchas* of Ath Cliath, Todd Lecture Series vii. 21.

29. *Boadan* is mentioned by the Four Masters in the year 861 (compare the Annals of Ulster, 862) as the herd of *Elcmar*, one of the princes of the *Tuatha Dé*. The ‘Cowhide’ is evidently the name of some landmark or notable object.

30. *a liac*. The letter *a* is added below the line: it may be an interpolation, as *liac* is scanned as a disyllable.

<summary>This page contains scholarly annotations for the poem "Brug I," clarifying linguistic forms, identifying mythological figures like the Dagda and Mac ind Oc, and locating geographical landmarks along the River Boyne.</summary>
<keywords>Brug na Bóinde, Mac ind Oc, Dagda, Irish mythology, philology, Book of Leinster, Leabhar na hUidhre, Boyne Valley, Boadan, Mátha</keywords>

--- Page 105 ---
<meta>This page continues the scholarly notes for the poem "Brug I," providing linguistic analysis and historical context for specific lines regarding the burial customs and legendary figures associated with the Brug na Bóinde.</meta>

<page-num>93</page-num>
<header>NOTES.</header>
<sig>BRUG I.]</sig>

33. I suppose *Ferta na Fáilenn* to be another local name: *foilenn* glosses *alcedo* <note>kingfisher</note>, Thes. Palæo-Hib. ii. 136, 231. The modern *faoileann* means ‘sea-gull.’ The word may here have a metaphorical application, like the “Wild Geese” of later days.
For *fertae* ‘trench’ (feminine singular), compare Thes. Palæo-Hib. ii. 259 *ad Ferti virorum Fece quam foderunt* etc. <note>to the Grave of the men of Fece which they dug</note>: ibid. 263 *hi Ferti virorum Feice* <note>in the Grave of the men of Feice</note>.

36. *forfein*: from *forbenim*. Finn was killed by Aichlech, one of the Luaigne, a tribe who lived near Tara: see Sil. Gadel. i. 91.

38. This Cellach is probably the son of Cormac whom Aengus Gaibuaidech killed for seducing his niece: Laws iii. 82; LU 53 a 41; *mellach* will therefore have the sense of ‘cajoling,’ which appears in the modern use of *meallaim*.

41. *barc*. The prose Dindshenchas (RC xv. 292) mentions the *Barc Crimthainn Nia Nair* <note>the Stronghold of Crimthann Nia Nair</note> among the ‘remarkable things of the Brug.’ *Barc*, like *long* <note>ship</note>, is applied metaphorically to houses: compare Temair iii. 57, 58 *long na lœch, frisanabar barc ban mbæth* <note>the ship of the warriors, which is called the ship of the foolish women</note> (Todd Lect. viii. 18). *Brainech* may mean ‘eminent’ or may denote the shape of the structure. *Tor* ‘tower,’ by a common metaphor, means ‘champion.’

42. *tonn* <note>wave</note> is preferable to *trom* (the reading of LU) as giving a response to *tromm* in line 41. In Irish (as in French) poetry, words of identical form are admissible rhymes only if they differ in meaning.

43. According to the *Senchas na Relec* <note>History of the Cemeteries</note>, all the kings of Tara down to Cremthann were buried at Cruachan. But “the nobles of the Tuatha De Danann were used to bury at the Brug . . . and Cremthann followed them because his wife Nar was of the Tuatha Dea” (Petrie, *Eccles. Archit.* 100–101).

45. I know nothing of Fintan Feradach.

51. *écradach*. Strachan (*Middle-Ir. Decl.* 18) has collected instances in which the predicative adjective is uninflected: compare line 77 *iatsom dimbúan* <note>they are transitory</note>.

53, 57. The *Senchas na Relec* explains why Cormac and Art were not buried in the Brug: (*Eccles. Archit.* 100–101).

54. *imman-aigtis luirg*: compare LL 50, 14 *immundaigtis buidne bend*. It might be better to render this as ‘around whom chariot-tracks were driven.’ If *lorg* ‘club’ ever followed the masculine declension, we might translate it as ‘on whom clubs were plied.’

70. *bés nirbo rom*. Compare *bes ni rom* LU 40 a 39. In both instances *bés* might be rendered ‘perhaps.’

<meta>This page continues the philological and historical notes for the poem "Brug I," focusing on linguistic reconstructions of specific terms and the identification of legendary figures and geographical locations mentioned in the text.</meta>

<page-num>94</page-num>
# NOTES. [BRUG I.

75. If *dersat* is correct, it must be the 3rd person plural present subjunctive of *dofichim* ‘I punish’; compare Strachan, Sigm. Fut. 4. The meaning then is general: if men persecute the faithful, they will suffer worse things themselves hereafter.
But it is tempting to read *dernsat*: we may then consider the collective *cúane* as the antecedent, and line 74 as a parenthesis, and translate: ‘a brood . . . that did not pay worship to the true God: so much the worse for them now!’

78. *slán* seems to mean ‘saved by their faith.’

81-84. I can make nothing of these lines. *Séil* may be the river Sele: compare *flumen Séle* <note>river Sele</note>, *Thes. Palæo-Hib.* ii. 263; and *Dinds.* of Tailtiu, 78 (LL 200 *b* 50). *Senbec ua Ebric* is mentioned (BB 308 *b* 27) as a *sai ecsidh* <note>an expert scholar</note>: according to O’Curry, *MC* ii. 50, he was a historian of the Tuatha Dé Danann. Perhaps in line 83 *uaibrig* should be *Ebric*. And *Cennindais* may be the place-name (now Kells).

85. *cond* ‘head’ seems here to mean ‘headquarters.’

86. The meaning of *dond* is doubtful: it is glossed by O’Davoren as *uasal nó brithem nó rígh* <note>noble or judge or king</note>.

87. *rán-tor* seems to be intended as a response to *ard-chon*.

<vocab>dersat, dofichim, dernsat, cúane, slán, Séil, flumen Séle, Thes. Palæo-Hib., Dinds. of Tailtiu, LL, Senbec ua Ebric, BB, sai ecsidh, Tuatha De Danann, uaibrig, Ebric, Cennindais, cond, dond, O’Dav., uasal nó brithem nó rígh, rán-tor, ard-chon</vocab>

The author provides linguistic conjectures for difficult passages, including potential corrections for specific Old Irish terms.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the philological and historical notes, specifically focusing on the second section of the text, Brug II. It provides commentary on legendary figures, word meanings, and historical associations related to the poem.</meta>

<header>BRUG II.] NOTES.</header>

### BRUG II.

15, 16. I have kept L’s text, as it can be defended; but the reading of all other <term>MSS.</term> *airm i ngénair . . . mar óen* is better.

17. *ben mic Námat.* Boand, wife of Nechtain. She bore Oengus to the Dagda. Her dog’s name was Dabilla (see the prose Dindshenchas). The poem (attributed to Cinad Ua Hartacáin), which begins in LL 209 *b* 25 and is continued at 208 *b* 1, tells the story of the birth of Oengus: how he was abandoned by his parents, and how Midir carried him off to Brí Léith (209 *a* 6); how he returned to seek out his rightful father, the Dagda, and prepared a feast for him; how the Dagda offered him a reward in return; and how Oengus won possession of the Brug from him through a verbal trick (209 *a* 50). The words *cialla cor* (25) seem to refer to this trick.

22. For *fríth* ‘discovery, find,’ compare *Sil. Gad.* i. 33, 10 *fó fríth fuaramar*; *LL* 147 *b* 40 *ní hinunn fríth fogeib cách*: *LU* 114 *b* 23 *ba hamrae in fríth.*

<margin>t</margin>
32. *Duma Treisc*, ‘the Barrow of Offal,’ named from the leftover food of the feast: compare *Tochm. Emire* (quoted by Meyer, index to *Aisl. M. C.*): ‘at the place where the refuse was thrown, he made a great hill of it: its name was Tresc in Máirimdill.’ O’Reilly has *treasg* ‘refuse,’ ‘chaff’; and *tresc* ‘dregs,’ ‘hogwash.’

34. *adba mbróin.* I have not found *adba* used as a neuter noun elsewhere; but the change from neuter to feminine is very common. All <term>MSS.</term> except L have *ba badba bróin*, which seems meaningless.

<margin>t</margin>
38. *a telctís cesta.* This seems to imply some kind of divination, but I can find no other reference to the place or the custom. Esclam, according to the prose Dindshenchas, was the Dagda’s brehon. Perhaps, then, we should translate it as ‘used to solve questions,’ but I have no example of *doléicim* used in this way.

41. It is difficult to determine the reading due to a lack of information regarding the incident referred to. L has *mba Buile* (not as in the facsimile *ṁba*): most of the other texts, and the prose, read *Buale* or *Buaile*. I can find nothing about this person; and lines 43 and 44 are therefore unclear to me. *lib* (43) is apparently an adjective. Dinneen has *lib* ‘dripping.’ I read *glund* in 44, assuming it to be the dative of *glond*: the meaning assigned is uncertain: see *Rev. Celt.* xxiv. 69. Perhaps *fiad-glond* means ‘honourable deed.’

45. This Cellach is called in the prose ‘son of Maelcoba’: he is, then, the Cellach who was the joint-king of Ireland with Conall and died at Brug na Boinne, according to the Four Masters, in 656 (653 in the Annals of Ulster). His partner Conall is said there to have been killed by Diarmaid, son of Aed Slaine: it seems, then, that Cellach was killed at the same time, and that the ‘pair’ referred to in line 47 are Diarmaid and his brother Blathmac, who took the kingship after the deaths of Conall and Cellach. There were different accounts of the death of Cellach: see AU 657, 663. The Cellach mentioned in Brug I line 38 is probably a completely different person.

<vocab>Brug na Boinne, Oengus, Dagda, Boand, Dindshenchas, Cellach, Cinad Ua Hartacáin, Middle Irish, sortilegium</vocab>

The notes analyze specific Middle Irish terms and compare various manuscript readings to clarify the text's meaning.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the philological and historical commentary on the poem Brug II, specifically focusing on notes for lines 48 through 96. It examines word etymologies, identifies historical figures like Cinaed and Oengus of Duleek, and references legendary events involving the Morrigan and Cuchulainn.</meta>

<header>NOTES. [BRUG II.</header>

48. I can only guess at the meaning of *bæthla*—if that is the true reading. Most of the <term>manuscripts</term> have *baethbla*, which O'Clery glosses as *baothbaile*.

50. *Cinaed*, son of Irgalach, was king of Ireland from 720 to 722 AD. 
*galma*: compare *Saltair na Rann* 5547, *galma* and *glóir*. O'Clery glosses the word as "hardness" (*crúas*).

53. *Currel*. Stokes in *Revue Celtique* xv. 292 translates this as "casket." However, in his edition of *The Destruction of Da Derga's Hostel*, he translates the phrase *cír chuirréil argit* as "a bright comb of silver," and suggests a derivation from **con-réil*. This may be correct, but it does not explain our passage. It seems to me that we are dealing with a different word here; *curel* meaning "coral" occurs in the Irish version of Marco Polo in *Zeitschrift für celtische Philologie* i. 384: O'Begley has *coiréal*. On the use of coral among the Celtic peoples, see S. Reinach in *Revue Celtique* xx.

56. For *ar chách*, see Meyer’s *Contributions*, under the entries *cách* and *achách*.

59. *gáela*. O'Reilly has *gaol* meaning "wound": compare *gáelim* ("I break") in *Saltair na Rann*. But perhaps we should refer the word to *gael* meaning "kinship," though the genitive is regularly *gaeil* (Atkinson, *Passions and Homilies*: Dinneen, *Dictionary*).

60. The word *sid* seems to imply that Aed Lurgnech belonged to the Tuatha Dé Danann, but I have found no other reference to him.

61, 62. *ruamna* means properly "redness": *cúan-bla* seems to be a compound of *cúan* ("troop") and *bla* ("place"): I understand *cliach* as the <term>genitive plural</term> of *cliú*.

64. This presumably refers to Cuchulainn wounding the Morrigan: see Miss Faraday's *The Cattle Raid of Cualnge*, pages 79–81. The ford where the fight took place has not been identified, but it cannot have been very far from Brug na Bóinne.

69. I suggest the reading *cátha*, regarding it as the plural of *cáith* ("husks" or "rubbish") in Meyer’s *Contributions*: here it refers to the debris of a skeleton. This provides a response to *Mátha* in line 70. 
Regarding this monster, see the *Dindshenchas* of Ath Cliath (*Revue Celtique* xv. 329, *Todd Lecture Series* vii. 20), where it is said to have been slain at "Lecc Bend on Brug mic ind Oc." The *Book of Leinster* gives the form *mátha* here and in lines 75 and 83: similarly, in Brug i. 25, *Lebor na hUidre* has *mátha*.

74. *toich* seems to be the word found in Würzburg Glosses 9, *a is toich dom* glossed as *est accepta mihi* ("it is acceptable to me"): see Windisch under the entry, and compare *Book of Leinster* 147 a 6, *in bé thondgel toich*.

88. The meaning seems to be that the barrow (called *Duma na Cnám* in the prose) belongs to the Matha whose memory it preserves.

89. The death of Oengus, son of Crundmael and abbot of Duleek, is recorded by the Four Masters in the year 778; in the *Annals of Ulster* in 782.

93. *Róot* is a two-syllable word here, rhyming with *Óoc*. The prose has *róut súla Midir* ("the path of Midir’s eye"): I know of no other allusion to the incident, except O'Clery's gloss under *ród*, and perhaps the verse in *Lebor na hUidre* 149 a 21.

95. *robriss* may be either transitive or intransitive.

96. *asidchói*: the <term>present subjunctive</term> of *as-cúad*: see Meyer’s *Contributions*.
