<header>IMBER N-AILBINE] NOTES.</header>
<sig>TODD LECTURE SERIES, VOL. IX. H</sig>

### IMBER N-AILBINE.

This poem was published from the Book of Ballymote (BB) by O'Curry, accompanied by a translation in *Atlantis*, vol. iv. 235.

The legend is told in the *Tochmarc Emire* (The Wooing of Emer): see *ZCP* iii. 243. The poet has drawn several phrases from this source, which are noted below.

The metre is extremely elaborate. All four lines of each stanza rhyme; the first and third lines end in monosyllables, while the second and fourth end in either disyllables or trisyllables. There are internal responses (sometimes double) between the first and second lines and between the third and fourth. Alliteration is strictly observed throughout.

1. *Muired.* The prose account states that Ruad was the grandson of the king of the Fir Muirid.

2. *do nach* seems to be used here in the construction illustrated by Atkinson in *Passions and Homilies* (PH) 817 *a*: *tailmire* (or *tuilmire*) is compounded from *toil* (will) and *mire* (madness or speed): compare the note on *talchar* in Ochan 62.

3. *trebthus* seems to mean "dwelling": compare *Mag Luirg* 1 (*Book of Leinster* 211 *a* 48) *Is eol dam im threibthas tó* ("It is known to me in my silent dwelling"); *Mag n-Aidni* 7 (*Book of Leinster* 156 *b* 40) *as mo threbthus co turad*. These passages support the reading found in the YH <note>possibly the Yellow Book of Lecan and the Book of Hy Many</note> manuscripts.

5. *rót-bla.* The second element of this compound seems to mean "noise" or "fame." The word appears in the *Book of Leinster* 10 *b* 25 in an obscure poem.

9, 10. It seems natural to consider *maith-mora* as a compound: this would involve writing, in line 9, *raith-roga* ("chief of courses"), using *raith* for *rith*.

10. *moch-maill*: compare *Druim Dairbrech* 18, and *Dinneen's Dictionary*.

13. *tri nóitib*: see also *ZCP* iii. 243 *teuora noaib*. The word *noithech* responds to *soithech*, but in Rath Essa 84 the *Book of Leinster* has *nóithech*; O'Clery does as well. The word appears eight times in *Saltair na Rann* without the mark of length: in *Saltair na Rann* 13 it rhymes with *immehloithech*, a word of doubtful derivation; none of the other passages requires a long vowel.

17. *lúd* is interpreted as "to stir from there in any direction": *as* = *inde* <note>Latin for "from that place"</note>.

21. *moslái*: see Windisch's *immlai*, and compare Faffand, 33. *tairdbe* ("cutting"); see the Glossary to the Laws.

22-24. These lines are corrupted in various ways across the different <term>manuscripts</term>. They must be corrected using the requirements of the metre. The only pair of end-rhymes provided are *sroth saoba* (H) with *dluth-choema* (RB). *bain-delba* (compare *ZCP* iii. 243) must be answered by *sain-delgna*. The second element of this word probably means "retirement," "separation," or something similar: compare *delgnaide* ("outlaw") in O'Reilly; *dealgnach* ("outlaw") in Dinneen; *delgnaim* in *Saltair na Rann* seems to mean "I distinguish." *sruth-sóeba*, which O'Clery glosses as .i. *saobchoire* ("whirlpool"), is probably the feminine genitive of a compound adjective (for *sruth-sóibe*) agreeing with *fairge*. I have therefore changed the order of the first three words, though perhaps the inversion was deliberately chosen for the sake of alliteration; if so, we may keep the reading *fairge tar sál*.

The author compares various manuscript readings, such as the Book of Leinster and the Book of Ballymote, to clarify difficult passages and technical terms.</summary>
<keywords>Imber n-Ailbine, O'Curry, Tochmarc Emire, Irish metre, philology, Ruad, Fir Muirid, Saltair na Rann, Book of Leinster, manuscripts</keywords>

--- Page 110 ---
<meta>This page continues the philological notes on the poem Imber n-Ailbine, focusing on specific line-by-line textual variants, word origins, and metrical analysis.</meta>

<header>NOTES. [IMBER N-AILBINE</header>

25, 26. *Fodroirgetar* is certainly sound: compare *Tochm. Em. ZCP iii. 243 atdam-natar dobatar se fotrergatar na longa.* But this provides no rhyme for *indsaigid*, so four lines must have been omitted. Furthermore, line 26 is short by one syllable in all texts except M, and M's *ba he fhad* is unintelligible. A response to *glé-alt* (see O'Clery) is required: perhaps we should read *ba hé fast*, which would also provide alliteration in line 26. O'Reilly has *fast* ‘a prison’: compare *fastaim* ‘I arrest.’

33. Compare *ZCP iii. 243 dobertatar noi longa oir ar noi n-oidcib dia fess leu.*

38. *malart*: this form (not *malairt*) is required to correspond with *alacht*. For the misspelling *maralt*, compare *SGI 84*, line 33: *iaram ba hálacht in ben: ropo máralt áirmiten*, where the word again has the sense of ‘altering for the worse’: compare Stokes’ O’Davoren, 1236.

39. *císal*. This word elsewhere is explained to mean ‘the devil’: here it perhaps has the sense of ‘sin’ or ‘harm.’

42. *tuinntib*: compare *Four Masters*, p. 2060 *x*, *rocheimnighsiot .. ina ttuinntibh tiugha toirtemhla 7 ina ndoiredhaibh dluithe dosccaoilte* ‘they proceeded in close and solid bodies and in compact, impenetrable squadrons’ (literally, ‘oakwoods’). The expression is evidently metaphorical, and *tuinntib* is perhaps literally ‘balls of thread’; hence ‘compact companies’: compare *toinnt* ‘thread,’ Coneys: *taoinnte* (and *tuinnte*) ‘thread,’ Dinneen.

43. *so-alt*: this compound occurs again in an unintelligible passage in the Book of Leinster (LL) 119 *a* 18. O’Clery has *soalt*, meaning a ‘good leap’; so also O’Reilly.

50. I have adopted the reading *fial-mod*, although it is supported only by two late manuscripts, because otherwise there is no internal response between lines 49 and 50: besides, the *fial mo* of RY points in this direction. The word is also found at LL 18 *b* 5. O’Clery has *mod*, meaning ‘man’: compare *Mide 7*. At BB 327 *b* 21 *mod* is explained as *cach ferda* (everything manly). If this curious passage contains anything more than the vagaries of a grammarian, the word may be a legacy from a pre-Goidelic population.

53. O’Clery has *eismeach* meaning ‘lying’ or ‘unready.’

60. Compare *ZCP iii. 244 Rocolatar ind fir ind amar ina nói umaidi.*

64. *setal-balc* for *selat-balc* ‘moment-strong’: for the metathesis compare *Carn uí Cathbad 41* (LL 199 *a* 56): BB 37 *b* 45: O’Clery's *seadal*.

66. *drongdige* is well supported and provides a good rhyme, but what does it mean?

75. *eacad* for *cocad*, answered by *-apad*.

78. *rige*: compare LL 157 *b* 33, *a rí rige*. O’Reilly has *righe* meaning ‘depredation.’

81. *tnú*. Most manuscripts read *trú*: if this is correct, it must be regarded as an abstract noun meaning ‘ill-luck.’

87. *tuiredach* is no doubt a derivative of *turid*, which is applied metaphorically to chieftains (compare line 57).

94. *toimsech*: a derivative of *tomus*. Compare *Ailech ii. 46* (Todd Lecture Series vii. 46); *Loch Dacháech 18* (BB 372 *b* 21).

<meta>This page transitions from notes on Imber n-Ailbine to the philological notes for the poem Ochan, continuing the analysis of manuscript variants and linguistic origins.</meta>

<header>NOTES ON OCHAN. 99</header>

### OCHAN.

The story of the killing of Niall of the Nine Hostages by his enemy Eochu, son of Enna Cendselach, is told in full in the tract edited by Meyer in *Otia Merseiana* ii. 84.

There is a copy of a different version of this tract at BB 134 *b* 27. It is prefaced there by an introductory notice of Eochu, in which lines 17–20 of our poem are quoted (BB 134 *b* 7). In this passage, as in LL, the poem is attributed to Cinæth ua Hartacain, who died in 973 (FM) or 974 (AU, Tigern.). K. Meyer (*Festschr. Wh. Stokes*) has edited an elegy on Niall. See also O'Mahony's Keating, 390 *seq*.

2. These adjectives can only refer to Niall, so the reading adopted gives a much better order than the *lettir* of L, which is supported by most manuscripts.

*dichmaig.* This word also occurs in SnR: compare LL 35 *b* 53, *debaid dichmaig*.

3. *slige* for *sliged*.

7. *roreraig* : for this use of *rigim* see Atk., *Lib. Hymn.* i. 262.

18. For *bág* 'pledge, alliance' see Meyer, *Contribb*. The line is quoted at BB 134 *b* 7 with *fri baig*.

22. *serig* : see SnR, index. *dúis* is explained by O'Dav. 682, first as *uasal* (noble) and then as *sét* (treasure). Compare V. Bran, index.

24. Compare BB 135 *a* 42 *romeabadar .vii. catha riana gnuis iarna egaib.*

26. *cen* gives no satisfactory sense, unless indeed it is to be interpreted with *atbert. tria rún* '(Eochu's) secret plot.'

30. *gáeth-rian*. O'Clery has *gaeth .i. fairge*. Compare *gaethamail* 'marshy,' *góith-luch* 'marsh,' Windisch. So *rian*, which is found only in LH, may be merely an intruding gloss. In line 26 *muir* has similarly found its way into the text of L. We may, then, omit *rian*, and keep *ba trom*.

31. So BB 135 *a* 15 *cóic geill Erend 7 geill Alban 7 Saxan 7 Breatan 7 Frange* : ibid. 26 *do dt'acallaim-si dodeacha[d]sa o Romancaib. Alla-sa [hi] eind coicidis dodtiefad a ngeill* : 'I have come from the Romans to speak with you, and this day fortnight their hostages will come to you.'

33. *Lótar* : for the plural verb with the collective *fian*, compare Almu ii. 9.

35. *brón baisse* = planctus <note>a striking of the hands in grief</note>: compare *bass* 'a smack with the hand' Meyer, *Contribb*.

39. *rodasní. Snīim* has two principal meanings: (1) 'I join, rivet, weave,' and (2) 'I vex.' The meaning might be either 'he had joined them together under his sovereignty' or 'he had caused them grief,' that is, by his death, or perhaps by his domination. The second meaning is required in the similar passage, Slige Dala 80 (LL 155 *b* 46) *is rempu sain, rodasní*; 'flying before them (he had angered them).' O'Clery has *rodasní .i. dosín*.

<summary>This page contains scholarly notes on the poem Ochan, focusing on the death of Niall of the Nine Hostages and providing linguistic analysis of specific Old and Middle Irish terms.</summary>
<keywords>Niall of the Nine Hostages, Eochu, Cinæth ua Hartacain, philology, Irish manuscripts, BB, LL, LH, K. Meyer, O'Clery</keywords>

<header>NOTES [MIDE</header>

41. *dosrimthais*: compare *doimmthastar* ‘is combined,’ Thes. Palæo.-Hib. ii. 50; *timmthastaib*, glossed as *fixis labris* (with fixed lips), *ibid.* ii. 56.

44. *díl* should perhaps be *díl* ‘fated end,’ echoing *dín* in 43. Peter O'Connell has *dín .i. críoch*: compare IT iii. 547: IT iv. index. But I doubt whether this is really a different word from *díl* ‘payment, remuneration.’

53. I read *thalman* against the <term>mss.</term>, for the sake of the rhyme with *adrann*.

57. *Cairenn*: mother of Niall, LL 33 *b* 23.

63. For *cóe* in the sense of *compitum* (crossroads) see Meyer, *Contributions*.

62. *tal-char* from *toil*: compare O'Reilly *tolchurthanach* (that is, *tolcharthanach*) ‘self-willed.’

---

# MIDE.

This poem is attributed in L to Aed ua Carthaig: compare line 49.

7. Compare O'Clery *glan an mod .i. glan an fear* (the man is pure).

11. *roatái*: see Kuhn's Zts. xxx. 98; Meyer, *Contributions*, under *adsúim*.

14. *fassad*, that is, *fossad* ‘truce’; apparently the fire was a symbol of peace, or the word may simply mean ‘a continuing, lasting,’ referring to the length of time: see O'Reilly *fosadh*. O'Clery has *asadh .i. adannad nó lasad* (kindling or lighting), quoting this line.

18. *anfót*: compare LL 125 *b* 55 *anfót do rád* (to speak heedlessly).

23. *muic finn* (white pig): explained by O'Clery under the entry *fionn*, as *muic lachdmhair mar atá cráin* (a milk-rich pig like a sow).

47. The best <term>mss.</term> give *adfét*; and possibly Windisch is right in suggesting that *adféit* or *ad fét* may be a contracted 3rd person plural present of *adfadaim*: though the editors of Thes. Palæo.-Hib. treat the word as singular in the passages cited by Windisch. The alternative is to read *adfiadat súidi snas* (or *a snas*); in either case *snas* probably refers to the cutting out of the druids' tongues.

51, 52. The meaning seems to be that God enjoins the king of Meath to take the poet under his protection.

<summary>This page contains philological and scholarly notes on two Irish poems, focusing on manuscript variants, word origins, and the poem "Mide" attributed to Aed ua Carthaig. The author analyzes specific terms related to Irish mythology and history, such as references to Niall and the ritual significance of fire.</summary>
<keywords>Mide, Aed ua Carthaig, Old Irish philology, manuscript variants, Niall of the Nine Hostages, Irish poetry, O'Clery, Windisch</keywords>

--- Page 113 ---
<meta>This page continues the philological notes on early Irish poetry, specifically transitioning from the poem Mide to the notes on Druim Dairbrech.</meta>

<header>DRUIM DAIRBRECH] NOTES. 101</header>

### DRUIM DAIRBRECH.

The metre of this poem must be carefully observed in determining the text. Every line ends with a disyllable: the second and fourth lines of each stanza rhyme fully; while they also contain responses and echoes to the first and third respectively.

According to the prose version, Dairbre belonged to the Aithech-Tuatha, who were defeated in the Battle of Commar by Tuathal Techtmar. It does not appear where Commar and Druim Dairbrech were situated.

The poem is attributed in the Book of Leinster to "F.": either Fulartach or Find may be intended.

7. *bas greimm*: literally, 'that will be a strength.'

10. *cuing* seems here to mean, like the Latin *jugum*, 'a hill or ridge.'

11, 12. I take Raigne to be a place-name: there is a Mag Raigne in Kilkenny (Annals of the Four Masters 859).

I can extract no sense from *mar*, the reading of the <term>manuscripts</term> in line 12. I conjecture *múr*, which may easily have been corrupted under the influence of *mar* in line 11. This reading also makes the position of *cath-garg* more natural. It follows that we must read *drech-deirg* in 12, against the <term>manuscripts</term>, and consequently *leth-leirg* in 10, following the Book of Leinster against all other <term>manuscripts</term>.

14. *sreth-chelg*. The rendering offered is a mere guess: for *celg* 'snare' see Meyer, *Contributions*: I take the word to denote one of those traps where a weight falls suddenly by the pulling of a string or chain; the image would be appropriate enough for the ambush laid by a guerrilla chief.

15. According to the prose, Dairbre was the son of Lulach and grandson of Ligmuine. *Túath Ligmuine* is mentioned as one of the *Aithech-tuatha*, Book of Ballymote 255 *a* 27.

18. *moch maill*: compare Inber Ailbine, 10.

17–24. Most of the tribes mentioned here occur in the enumeration of the Aithech-tuatha at Book of Ballymote 255 *a* 11, 19, 20, 25, 26.

20. *gád-méin* is required to rhyme with *lán-réir*: it is a compound of *gád*, 'need,' a word which is sometimes confused with *gábud*, 'danger.' The meaning seems to be that the pangs of hunger drove the *Aithech-tuatha* into rebellion.

22. *bán-dír*. The long vowels are required by the rhyme. For this use of *bán* compare *bán-gleó*, Saltair na Rann 3038, and *bán-béim*, Cath Ruis na Ríg, 115.

25. *fuilid*. I have not met this form elsewhere: *fuilide* is found in Saltair na Rann, and *Ériu*, ii. 56.

27. *mongach* appears to be a derivative of *mong*, 'moorland, fen,' etc., according to Dinneen.

30. *ní ro-n-deg-therb*, if sound, seems to mean 'he did not fortunately separate himself.'

36. *dídail* may be the infinitive of a compound *di-in-dlung. The word occurs in *Thesaurus Palæohibernicus* ii. 295, where it is translated as 'reproach.'

<vocab>Druim Dairbrech, Aithech-Tuatha, Tuathal Techtmar, Raigne, Lulach, Ligmuine, gád-méin, Dinneen, Thesaurus Palæohibernicus</vocab>

<summary>This page provides linguistic and historical commentary on the poem Druim Dairbrech, focusing on its metrical structure, the tribal history of the Aithech-Tuatha, and specific manuscript variations. The author offers corrections to the text based on rhyme schemes and provides definitions for technical Irish terms used in the verses.</summary>
<keywords>Druim Dairbrech, Aithech-Tuatha, Tuathal Techtmar, Irish philology, manuscript analysis, place-names, Raigne, metrical analysis, linguistic commentary</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the philological notes on Irish topographical poems, specifically focusing on the sections titled Lagin I, Lagin II, and the beginning of Sliab Bladma.</meta>

<header>NOTES. [SLIAB BLADMA</header>

# LAGIN I.

The incidents alluded to here are fully told in *Orgain Dind Ríg*: see ZCP iii. 1. Cobthach murdered his brother Loegaire, King of Ireland, and afterwards Loegaire's son, Ailill Aine: cf. 27. Labraid Loingsech, also called Moen, son of Ailill Aine, avenged his father by burning Cobthach at Dind Rig, along with thirty kings: cf. 10.

12. *cóic cét*. The verses quoted in the *Orgain Dind Ríg*, ZCP iii. 8, give the date as *trí chét mblíadan re ngein Crist*: but compare ZCP iii. 14, where another version has *cóic* <note>cét</note>. The Four Masters give the date as 542 <term>B.C.</term>

22. *Láigne* is properly the <term>nominative plural</term> of *láigen* ‘lance.’ S₂ reads *ráidter Lagin fri Laigne*, which is simpler grammar, but is unsupported.

# LAGIN II.

The first and third stanzas of this poem are quoted by the glossator of the Amra Columcille in the Trinity College copy of the *Liber Hymnorum*: they were translated by Atkinson in *L. Hymn.* ii. 58. All three verses are translated by Stokes in the *Bodleian Dindshenchas*, p. 7. The first stanza occurs again in another poem, LL 19 *a* 23: the second is quoted in the *Cóir Anmanna*, IT iii. 364.

There is a second copy of the poem at LL 277 *a* 13, where an additional stanza is inserted after line 4:—

> A tuaim Tenba traǽta tuirc,
> robith Cobthach in caolcuirp:
> odhin (?) ceol consmbert (?) hi bhus,
> ised (?) consert ar coibnus.

> ‘At Tuaim Tenba chieftains were overcome; Cobthach of the lean body was slain; since by means of the music I conceived (?) here, it is that which joined our union.’

Compare the second couplet with <term>cp.</term> ZCP iii. 5, 6.

14. The same number is given in the *Orgain Dind Ríg*, ZCP iii. 14: in Lagin I. 18 it is three thousand.

# SLIAB BLADMA.

5. *rot*. This word occurs in Cath Muige Ratha 40, rhyming with *cloc, boc*: O'Donovan translates it as ‘daring’; <term>cf.</term> LL 150 *b* 31 *rot a ngal*. The literal meaning is perhaps ‘red’: Cormac, under the entry <term>s. v.</term> *rotta*, has *ar is rot cech nderg*. So *rúad* means both ‘red’ and ‘fiery.’

6. *Herot*. Bury, in the *Engl. Hist. Review*, 1902, pp. 263–4, connects this place with the *ríg Hirotae* of L. Ardm. 14 *r b*. In line 11 the name is treated as a feminine: perhaps we should read *Heruit* there, and suppose it to be a <term>u-stem</term> of uncertain gender.

<vocab>Cobthach, Loegaire, Labraid Loingsech, Dind Ríg, Orgain Dind Ríg, Lagin, Sliab Bladma, Amra Columcille, Liber Hymnorum, Dindshenchas, Cóir Anmanna, Cath Muige Ratha, Cormac's Glossary</vocab>

<meta>This page continues the philological and explanatory notes for the Irish topographical poems, specifically moving from the notes on Sliab Bladma to the section on Fid Ngaibli.</meta>

<header>FID NGAIBLI] NOTES.</header>

22. *ban-tolcaib* may be related to *toilc* ('pride') according to Windisch; but it is more probably the word translated as 'breach' in *IT* iii. 533. Compare *SnR* 6767 *co tolcaib na tor talctrén* and LL 258 *a* 48; 101 *a* 13, etc. Dinneen lists *tolg* ('force, effort') and *tolgaim* ('I tear, smash').

23, 24. Due to a lack of independent information regarding the incident mentioned, the true meaning of this passage remains unclear. *Crad* is perhaps written for *crod*, which can mean either 'cattle' or 'destruction' (see Meyer’s *Contributions*).

27. *tánic*: singular form used instead of the plural.

---

### FID NGAIBLI.

The name survives in the Feeguile, a stream that joins the River Barrow near Monasterevin. We are dealing here with a myth that is imperfectly reported and, consequently, unintelligible. The prose version says that Ainge was the daughter of the Dagda; however, from line 9, it would appear instead that he was the father of the girl for whom the *drochta* was made. For parallels regarding the mysterious sympathy between this object and the tide, see Miss Hull's article on *Geasa* in *Folklore*, 1901, p. 51.

2. The best manuscripts read *flescach*, but this creates a poor rhyme.

3. *luaige* seems to be an abstract noun derived from *lúag*; compare *luaigim* ('I buy') in the index of *Lives of Saints from the Book of Lismore*.

6. The prose states *ní anad de thinsaitin céin nobíd in muir for línad* (compare line 11), and *ní ticed banne ass céin ba haithbe* (LL 159 *b* 2). Presumably, then, *taidbe* equals *aithbe*, although I have not encountered the word used in this sense elsewhere.

<margin>7. *timme* ('abridgment')? Compare *gan brón báis agus time saoghail d'imirt air* ('without the sorrow of death and the shortening of life being inflicted on him'), *Ossianic Society* iii. 92 (quoted by Windisch); and compare this with the phrase *rothimdibsit a saeghul* ('they cut short his life'), *IT* i. 130, 19. Compare also our passage with Rath Essa 7, *cét cech mil centimdibe* ('a hundred of every animal without reduction'). See further LL 119.*b* 16 and *SGI* 68, line 41.</margin>

9. *dognid* is written for *dogníth*, corresponding with *gním*. For *drochta*, see *ZCP* iii. 468; it may mean a kind of boat in this context.

12. *nodiled*. I have no other instance of this verb, but it seems to be connected with *dil* or *dile*.

13. *dafall*: the <term>preterite</term> of *tallaim*.

14. Gaible is called Gabol in line 1; similarly, in the Bodleian prose, he bears both names, while in the Rennes version he is called Gaible.

19. *rotháid* seems to be the <term>preterite</term> of a verb *táidim*; compare *táid* ('thief') and *táide* ('stealth').

<summary>This page provides philological notes and commentary on the Irish poem Fid Ngaibli, discussing word origins, manuscript variations, and the mythological context of the Feeguile stream.</summary>
<keywords>Fid Ngaibli, Feeguile, River Barrow, Ainge, Dagda, drochta, Irish mythology, philology, etymology</keywords>

--- Page 116 ---
<meta>This page continues the philological and explanatory notes for the topographical poems, moving from the discussion of Fid Ngaibli to the sections concerning Mag Life, Berba (the River Barrow), and Moin Gái Glais.</meta>

<header>NOTES. [MOIN GÁI GLAIS</header>

### MAG LIFE.

It should be noted that Life is properly the name of the plain through which the Liffey flows; the river is usually called *aba Life*.

3. *gaire* seems to mean the piety with which her memory is kept alive.
6. *chélig*. Compare the note on Rath Esa 42.
8. According to the prose version, Deltbanna, son of Drucht (that is, Dewdrop, son of Dew) was the spencer to Conaire, and married Life.

### BERBA.

Now the River Barrow.
2. *Mag n-Ailbe* was a plain in the south of Kildare on the east bank of the Barrow: FM 906.
4. By substituting *bláth* for *bláith* from the <term>MSS.</term>, we obtain a response to *fáth* and a better sense.
5. The prose explains that Diancecht (or MacCecht) killed Mechi, the son of the Morrigan, burnt his three hearts which contained three serpents, and cast the ashes into the Berba.
7. *athbach*, from *athbongim*, would naturally mean ‘a second stroke’; the meanings given by Meyer in *Contributions*, under 1, 2, and 3, seem to be approximations from various glossators. Here, the meaning may be either that a ‘second stroke’ was not required, or, more probably, that ‘recovery’ was impossible. This would accord with P. O’C.’s *.i. aithearrach* and O’R.’s ‘renewal.’
9. The prose says there were ‘the shapes of three serpents’ in Mechi’s heart; here they are described as the coils of a single snake.
11. *óg n-ell*. For this use of *óg* as a noun, compare *óg mo charat Lia Nothain* 31 (BB 393 *b* 48); *óg cána, Cáin Adamnáin* 29.
16. *Cŭan* seems to be written for *cŭain*, the dative of *cŭan* (‘pack’), in order to rhyme with *bŭan*. This provides a much better sense than can be extracted from *cŭan* meaning ‘haven.’

### MOIN GÁI GLAIS.

The incidents referred to here are told more fully in the prose version.
8. *daiger* is translated as “javelin” by O’Donovan in *Magh Rath* 152. It is probably the English word ‘dagger,’ and is distinct from *daigir* meaning ‘fire’ (IT iv. index).
10. *fola* for *fala*: compare *IT* iv. index; M'Sweeney, *Caithr. C. C.*, p. 3, note.
11. *tola*: the accusative plural of *tul*, which properly means the ‘boss of a shield.’
12. *Aith* appears to be a proper name; none of the <term>MSS.</term> has a mark of length over this word, nor over *taith* in line 11.
13. The prose states that Culdub was the champion of Fiachu Sraiptine.
15. O’Cl. has *ba greit gáid*, meaning *ba gaisgeadach i ngábhad* (“he was a warrior in danger”). Possibly we should write *dilim*, a word which occurs in *Cáin Adamnáin*, § 52, and may mean ‘compensation’ or something similar.

The author provides etymological analysis of Old Irish terms and reconciles the poetic text with prose versions of the myths.</summary>

<keywords>Mag Life, Liffey, Berba, River Barrow, Diancecht, Mechi, Morrigan, Moin Gái Glais, Irish mythology, philology, Fiachu Sraiptine, Old Irish etymology</keywords>

<header>FAFFAND] NOTES.</header>

### FAFFAND.

Of the personages mentioned in this poem, Meilge is the only one of whom anything seems to be known from other sources. He expelled his cousin Labraid Loingsech from the high-kingship of Ireland: see the *Four Masters, in the year of the world* 4658–4694. Meilge is called “King of Beirre” (35), and “Meilge of Imlech” (24). As Broccaid is called in the prose version “Broccaid of the Galians of Labraid Loingsech,” we must consider him to belong to Meilge’s enemies, which explains the persecution of his family.

1. *co n-gním gíall* ‘with deed of hostages,’ that is, prowess by which hostages are won: *compare nert-gíallach* ‘winning hostages by force,’ *LL* 199 *b* 39.

2. *gorm-glan*. O’Davoren 1033 has *gorm .i. urdaire*: *compare gorm-sluag*, *LL* 158 *a* 5, *gorm-ríg*, *Martyrology of Oengus*.

4. For *taithme* or *taichme*, *compare* Carmun 7 (*LL* 215 *a* 34) *tictis dia thaichme*, where O’Curry (*Manners and Customs vol. iii.* 529) translates ‘came to celebrate it’: Bend Boguine 2 (*BB* 397 *a* 22) *hi taichme treb tuaige*. In all these passages the manuscripts vary between *taichme* and *taithme*. The latter form is preferable if the word is connected with *taithmim*, which occurs in Laws v. 322, 24 (the same passage repeated at 338, 13), where Atkinson translates ‘contemplate.’ *taithme* seems to be a shorter form of *taithmet* ‘remembrance,’ according to Windisch.

17. *or i n-or*: *compare Four Masters* page 116, 16, ‘*ba han tra baoi crioch 7 torann Ereann or ind or.*’

22. *sernad* I suppose to be the verbal noun from O’Reilly’s *sernaim* ‘I loose, untie’: *compare Windisch sernim* (1).

<margin>conjecture
highly
obscure</margin>
<margin>☩</margin> 27. *cen chuiscle*. O’Reilly has ‘private affair, sorcery’; and something of this latter sense appears to be present in the passages LL 197 *a* 41 and LL 157 *b* 32: in the former, it is applied to the hidden lower limbs of sirens; in the latter, to secret and magical knowledge. In the present instance, the reference seems to be to the magical arts by which Aige was transformed into a bubble of water: see the prose version, *ní fríth di acht bolg uisci* <note>nothing of her was found but a bubble of water</note>. Read then *co cuiscle*.

31. *ní tairm cen taig*, literally ‘not a fame without right.’ I suppose *taig* to be a spelling of *toich*.

38. The prose version says, ‘three blotches were raised’ on Meilge by Faifne’s satire: *compare LL.* 81 *a* 40 *go tócbaitis teora bolga bar a agid, ail 7 anim 7 athis*.

<summary>This page contains philological and historical notes on the poem "Faffand," identifying the high-king Meilge and his political rivalries. It provides linguistic analysis of specific Old Irish terms and relates them to other historical manuscripts and the prose versions of the Dindsenchas.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the philological notes from the previous section, moving from the discussion of Faffand to the notes for the poem regarding Almu.</meta>

<header>NOTES. [ALMU I.</header>

39. *sethad* seems to be a genitive; but if so, I have no instance of the word. It can hardly be the same that occurs in *Revue Celtique* volume 15, page 311, *x*, *ic sethad in buair*. O'Reilly has *seith*, *seitheadh* "a skin," "hide"; but this is no doubt merely a misspelling of *seche*.

43. *segma*. This word is also found in the Book of Ballymote, page 297, column a, line 31, *ri segma*.

45. *rosedlad*. Compare *Revue Celtique* volume 16, page 44, *rosedlad buinde a sliasta* "his shin-bone was fractured" (?) according to Stokes; also *Tipra Sengarmna* 123 (Book of Leinster, page 197, column b, line 52) *sedlais co sæb iarsaine Crimthand Cæl*.

50. *immasclaig* seems to be a preterite, formed presumably from **imm-ad-con-sligim*.

51, 52. The natural syntactic order seems to be *combeth a ainm sír fri gairm in tsendumai*; but because *fria* was written, anticipating the dependent genitive, the nominative in *sen-duma* is added by way of explanation, just as appositional phrases are commonly put in the nominative without regard to the case of the noun to which they relate.

53. *tibri* "laughter" seems curiously inappropriate; but I can find no more suitable meaning for the word.

## ALMU I.

<term>ALMU</term>, now the Hill of Allen in Kildare, was the chief stronghold of the Fianna. In the later Ossianic ballads, it is usually called Almhain. For the story, see the *Fotha Catha Cnucha*, edited by Hennessy in *Revue Celtique* volume 2, page 86, from the *Lebor na hUidre*, and printed by Windisch in his *Kurzgefasste Grammatik* [Concise Grammar], page 121. See also the *Macgnimartha Find* in the Ossianic Society volume 4, page 288; reprinted by Kuno Meyer in *Revue Celtique* volume 5, page 195. *Fotha Catha Cnucha* agrees with this poem closely for the most part, but adds the origin and outcome of the feud between Finn mac Cumaill and Goll mac Morna.

Stanzas 1, 2, 4, and 5 of our poem are also found incorporated in *Acallam na Senórach*: *Irische Texte* volume 4, page 36. However, a different couplet is substituted for lines 15 and 16.

13. At Book of Leinster page 50, line 13, Tadc is called the son of Nuadu Necht; but this must be wrong, as Nuadu Necht was the son of Setna Sithbacc, king of Leinster (*Silva Gadelica* volume 2, page 519 (iv) a), whereas our Nuadu was the son of Achi and the druid to Cathair Mor (*Fotha Catha Cnucha*). This confusion is repeated in the document quoted in *Silva Gadelica* volume 2, page 519 (iv) b.

15. *almu*. At line 19, we have *alamain*: compare *Fotha Catha Cnucha*, *rocomled almu*.

25-28. These lines are metrically defective: *thrén* and *mun-cháem* make a poor rhyme. Line 26 has one syllable too few in the manuscript, and line 27 has one too many.

<keywords>Almu, Hill of Allen, Fianna, Finn mac Cumaill, Nuadu Necht, Irish philology, Fotha Catha Cnucha, manuscript analysis</keywords>

<header>ALEND] NOTES. 107</header>

31. *can chert is can chlód*. As D'Arbois points out (*Revue Celtique* volume 21, page 247), the meaning is that Cumall neither obtained Tadc's consent, nor established his right by defeating Tadc in a duel. Compare the *dindschenchas* of Snám dá Én, where Conan fights Aed Rind for his daughter Ceilg: Book of Leinster, page 203, column b, line 39 and following.

> ->Luid Conán, ba crúaid in fis,<-
> ->co fuair Aed Rind ina liss,<-
> ->co rochunnig fair cert claidib<-
> ->no ingin co imdemin.<-

59. *Fotha Catha Cnucha* section 6 has "**rodíúlt a athair di ocus nir léic cuci hí, ar robo torrach hí, ocus asbert fria muntir a breoad ocus arai nir lam ammudugud fri Cond.**"

60. 'them': specifically Murni and her child.

62-72. The **manuscript** is partly illegible here; in line 62 a two-syllable word has been lost: the meaning required is 'white, fair,' etc. In line 68 *b* may be the first letter of *Bendron*: compare *Fotha Catha Cnucha*, section 6. But in the *Macgnimartha Find*, Bodmall is called a *ban-drui* (female druid): perhaps *Bendron* is a corruption of this.

## ALMU II.

**This** composition appears to be made up of scraps of different pieces tagged together.

10. *Fiann*. All texts except manuscript E read *fian*. Whether the two forms are originally distinct or not (regarding which see Zimmer, *Zeitschrift für deutsche Altertumskunde* 35, 52), they are certainly constantly confounded in our **manuscripts**: see, for example, Faffand 47. The word must here be a collective noun with a plural verb.

12. *gú* for *gáe* or *gái* (spear). The forms *gó, góo, gou* also occur.

16. This is an attempt to explain the name Almu (accusative case Almain) by connecting it with *ailemain*, the verbal noun of *ailim* (to foster). Other equally worthless etymologies will be found in the prose *Dindschenchas*.

## ALEND.

**Alend**, now Knockaulin, in Kildare, was one of the principal strongholds of the kings of Leinster.

**This** poem consists principally of a string of titles by which the place might be described, in memory of various chieftains who had possessed it.

2. Art Messdelmond was the son of Setna Sithbacc: see the prose version.

7, 8. Setna Sithbacc was the son of Lugaid, the son of Bresal Brec (*Silva Gadelica* volume 2, page 519 (iv.) a).

7. For this use of *luam* 'pilot,' compare *luam gaiscid géir* (pilot of sharp weapons), Book of Leinster, page 195, column b, line 55. *letrad cailc* means 'the shield was hacked.'

<summary>This page provides philological and genealogical notes for the poems Almu II and Alend, discussing the etymology of place names and referencing various Middle Irish manuscripts and historical sources.</summary>
<keywords>Alend, Almu, Knockaulin, Dindschenchas, Cumall, Tadc, Irish philology, Middle Irish, manuscripts, Leinster, Fotha Catha Cnucha, Setna Sithbacc, Macgnimartha Find</keywords>

--- Page 120 ---
<meta>This page continues the philological and historical notes on the poem regarding Alend (Knockaulin), specifically identifying individuals and locations mentioned in the text.</meta>

<header>NOTES. [ALEND</header>

8. The grammar is uncertain. Perhaps we should adopt the reading *forad úa Setnai*, meaning ‘the seat of the grandsons of Setna.’

9. *Messdelmond*, that is, Art: see the note on line 2.

12. No doubt the person intended is Messgegra, king of Leinster, who appears in the *Talland Etair* (RC volume 8, page 47).

16. The reading is uncertain. I know nothing of Andrithir (or Maenindir), if indeed it is a proper name.

17. Fergus Fairge was the son of Nuadu Necht and the father of Ross Ruad, whose son Find is mentioned in line 19: see the Book of Leinster (LL) 346, column 6.

20. Bresal Bregaman is perhaps identical to Bresal Brec, the grandfather of Setna Sithbacc, in *Silva Gadelica* volume 2, page 519 (iv.) *a*.

<margin>Latin</margin>
21. I have not found the name Luchdond among the Leinster genealogies. It is perhaps an epithet rather than a proper name. In *Fled Bricrend* 22, 46, it is applied to Loegaire Buadach: Thurneysen, in *Sagen aus dem alten Irland*, page 35, translates it as ‘Mäusehaut’ (*luch-thond*, meaning ‘mouse-skin’).

25. *bethir bethrach*, as in the Book of Leinster (LL) 146 *b* 19: compare LL 247 *a* 16 *ruathar bethrach*, and see Meyer, *Contributions*.

30. *trét* (‘herds’) is used here to mean *slúag* (a host or army).

33. Cathair Mor left Alend to Fiachu Baicid: see *Leabhar na gCeart*, page 203.

35. The manuscripts point to *rígu*: we should rather expect *ríge*, meaning ‘by his kingship.’

36. *nenaise* means ‘made captive.’ But why should Fiachu be fighting against the ‘champions of Alend’? Perhaps the reference is to the custom of chaining the fighting ranks together, as in *Cath Muige Rátha*, page 178: compare Joyce, *Social History of Ancient Ireland*, volume 1, page 144.

37. Bresal Beolach was the son of Fiachu Baicid: see the Annals of the Four Masters (FM) 435.

41. *nodassaig*: from the verb *adsagim*.

42. Sruthar Segsa, that is, the Boyne, which rises in Sid Nechtain, *Revue Celtique* volume 15, pages 315, 334.

48. *adclaidim* usually means ‘hunt’: but the prose version here has *conacclaid mur*: compare Meyer, *Contributions*, under *concladim*.

51. *rind* is regarded here as a genitive plural: but it is perhaps an adjective: compare *Aed Rind* in the Book of Leinster (LL) 203 *a* 30: *a rigi rabartaig rind* in the Book of Ballymote (BB) 58 *a* 36.
